<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:nls"></a>
Consider the defocusing nonlinear Schrödinger equation $$\label{eq:nls}
 i\partial_tu+\Delta u=|u|^{p-1}u
 \qquad\text{on }\mathbb T^d=(\mathbb R/2\pi\mathbb Z)^d,$$ where $p\geq3$ is odd. Its scaling-critical Sobolev exponent is $s_{\mathrm{cr}}=d/2-2/(p-1)$. In the energy-supercritical range $s_{\mathrm{cr}}>1$, the positive conserved energy does not control the regularity required for classical continuation. This leaves open the possibility that singularities persist even for very regular data. For odd $p$, the Sobolev algebra property and a Duhamel fixed point give local well-posedness in $H^k(\mathbb T^d)$ for $k>d/2$. Foundational Euclidean Cauchy theories include (Ginibre and Velo 1979; Cazenave and Weissler 1990) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[GinibreVelo1979](#ref-GinibreVelo1979), [CazenaveWeissler1990](#ref-CazenaveWeissler1990)]. Boundedness of the critical Sobolev norm implies global scattering in several whole-space supercritical regimes (Killip and Visan 2010) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[KillipVisan2010](#ref-KillipVisan2010)]. Neither fact supplies an a priori critical-norm bound from the conserved energy. Bourgain surveyed global existence, blowup, and long-time dynamics (Bourgain 2000) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Bourgain2000](#ref-Bourgain2000)]. Our result gives a robust deterministic singularity in one such equation.

<a id="stmt-thm-blowup"></a>
<div id="thm:blowup" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (An open set of blowup data). *There exist an odd integer $p\geq3$, a finite integer $k>8$, and a nonempty open set $\mathcal U\subset H^k(\mathbb T^{12};\mathbb C)$ with the following property. For every $u_0\in\mathcal U$, the unique local solution of <a href="#eq:nls" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nls">[eq:nls]</a> extends to $$u\in C([0,T);H^k)\cap C^1([0,T);H^{k-2})$$ for some finite $T>0$, but has no continuous $H^k$ extension through $T$. More precisely, there are $x_*\in\mathbb T^{12}$ and $c_0>0$ such that $$\lim_{t\uparrow T}(T-t)^{1/(p-1)}|u(t,x_*)|=c_0.$$ The power can be chosen sufficiently large, and $k$ is then fixed after that choice.*

</div>

The prescribed-Gaussian question is motivated by the energy-supercritical branch of Deng–Nahmod–Yue’s Open problem 3 (Deng, Nahmod, and Yue 2020, sec. 9.2.3, p. 98) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[DengNahmodYue2020](#ref-DengNahmodYue2020)]. The eventual-$\alpha$, $H^m$ quantifiers below are the formulation considered here, not a verbatim restatement of that problem. We next state the probabilistic consequence with its quantifiers. Let $g_n$, $n\in\mathbb Z^d$, be independent standard complex Gaussian variables, and let $\langle n\rangle=(1+|n|^2)^{1/2}$.

<a id="stmt-cor-gaussian"></a>
<div id="cor:gaussian" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Failure of universal Gaussian global existence). *For the fixed power $p$ and index $k$ in Theorem <a href="#thm:blowup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:blowup">1</a>, and for every $\alpha>k+6$, the initial datum <a id="eq:gaussian"></a>$$\label{eq:gaussian}
 u_0^\omega(x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb Z^{12}}
       g_n(\omega)\langle n\rangle^{-\alpha}e^{in\cdot x}$$ belongs to $H^k$ almost surely and has positive probability of finite-time blowup. In particular, the following assertion is false: for every energy-supercritical pair $(d,p)$ there is a finite $\alpha_0(d,p)$ such that, for every $\alpha>\alpha_0(d,p)$ and every $$d/2+2<m<\alpha-d/2,$$ the local $H^m$ solution with the Gaussian datum extends almost surely to $C(\mathbb R;H^m(\mathbb T^d))$.*

</div>

The probability in this corollary may depend on $\alpha$. We do not claim probability-one blowup or a lower probability bound uniform in $\alpha$. A single fixed pair $(d,p)$ is enough to refute the universal assertion, and an explicit numerical value of the permissible power is not needed.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### Context.

An earlier counterexample for a specially designed vector-valued, forced defocusing Schrödinger system showed that conservation and scaling alone cannot settle scalar global regularity (Tao 2018, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Tao2018](#ref-Tao2018)]. For the unforced scalar energy-supercritical equation, Merle, Raphaël, Rodnianski, and Szeftel (Merle et al. 2022, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[MRRS2022](#ref-MRRS2022)] obtain radial whole-space type-II blowup on a finite-codimension family for the pairs $(5,9),(6,5),(8,3),(9,3)$, subject to a stated profile nondegeneracy condition. Cao-Labora, Gómez-Serrano, Shi, and Staffilani (Cao-Labora et al. 2024, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[CLGSS2024](#ref-CLGSS2024)] construct periodic nonradial blowup for the cubic equation in dimension eight, again on a finite-codimension family. Those constructions use hydrodynamic profiles with spatial scale $(T-t)^{1/r}$, where $r>2$. More recently, Buck, Carballeira, Gómez-Serrano, and Shi (Buck et al. 2026, Theorem 1.1 and Section 4.3) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BuckEtAl2026](#ref-BuckEtAl2026)] construct asymptotically self-similar scalar defocusing blowup at the Schrödinger scale from smooth radial, compactly supported data on $\mathbb R^d$ for $(d,p)=(3,27),(4,9),(5,7)$. Their computer-assisted profile matching and nonlinear stability yield a finite-codimension family; their theorem does not assert a physical Sobolev-open blowup set.

Here the spatial scale is $(T-t)^{1/2}$, the amplitude is $(T-t)^{-1/(p-1)}$, and there is a logarithmic phase rotation. This is the meaning of self-similar, or type-I, blowup in this paper. The open neighborhood in Theorem <a href="#thm:blowup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:blowup">1</a> is essential for Corollary <a href="#cor:gaussian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:gaussian">2</a>: full support of a Gaussian measure charges every nonempty open set, whereas a finite-codimension existence theorem alone does not give this conclusion. We prove the profile, spectral stability, periodic localization, and openness needed here directly; the cited blowup constructions provide historical and methodological context, not inputs to those proofs.

Physical-data openness has precedents for the focusing equation. Merle, Raphaël, and Szeftel (Merle, Raphaël, and Szeftel 2010, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[MRS2010](#ref-MRS2010)] construct an $H^1$-open set of self-similar blowup data for slightly mass-supercritical, energy-subcritical powers in dimensions one through five. Under a mode-stability hypothesis, Li (Li 2024, Theorems 1.4–1.5) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Li2023](#ref-Li2023)] obtains asymptotic stability and $H^1$-open blowup sets by matching symmetry parameters to a stable graph. Here the spectral classification and parameter matching are proved for the defocusing profile and the periodic evolution.

The probabilistic conclusion differs from global-flow results for particular invariant ensembles in periodic and supercritical NLS (Bourgain 1994; Sy 2021; Gueye, Longmou-Moffo, and Sy 2025) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Bourgain1994](#ref-Bourgain1994), [Sy2021](#ref-Sy2021), [GueyeEtAl2025](#ref-GueyeEtAl2025)]. The Gaussian law in Corollary <a href="#cor:gaussian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:gaussian">2</a> has full support in the stated $H^k$ space; its positive-probability blowup event follows from the deterministic physical-data openness, not from stochastic forcing or a random-data construction of singular solutions.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
###### Structure and methods.

Write $a=1/(p-1)$. As $a\to0$, a radial profile develops a flat core with positive limiting pressure and a free exterior. This limit turns the spectral matching problem into a pair of explicitly defined confluent hypergeometric solutions. A Laguerre recurrence gives analytic tail bounds, including at a removable singular parameter. Exact integer and rational certificates then count all low-angular roots and produce a nonzero profile-matching degree; a complex-path multiplier treats all remaining angular degrees. Section <a href="#sec:free" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:free">2</a> establishes these free facts, and Appendix <a href="#cert:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:arithmetic">7</a> gives the finite arithmetic recipes and their reproducible certificate. Matched asymptotics for focusing NLS profiles appear in (Bahri, Martel, and Raphaël 2021) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[BMR2021](#ref-BMR2021)]; rigorous computer-assisted focusing profile constructions appear in (Dahne and Figueras 2026; Donninger and Schörkhuber 2026) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[DahneFigueras2026](#ref-DahneFigueras2026), [DonningerSchorkhuber2026](#ref-DonningerSchorkhuber2026)], with exact fraction arithmetic in the latter. Buck and coauthors (Buck et al. 2026) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[BuckEtAl2026](#ref-BuckEtAl2026)] use validated interval arithmetic for the defocusing profile in different dimensions. The present large-power limit and finite certificates are proved here for the profile and full angular spectrum required by our theorem.

Section <a href="#sec:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:profiles">3</a> constructs genuine finite-power profiles and the uniform bounds needed for the spectrum. Section <a href="#sec:spectrum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:spectrum">4</a> shows that the only modes in the relevant half-plane are the phase, twelve translations, and blowup time. Two points are central: a uniform outgoing estimate excludes modes escaping to large temporal or angular frequency, and compact convergence of a constrained pressure problem preserves algebraic multiplicity in the bounded spectral region. For slightly mass-supercritical focusing NLS, Li identifies the low-energy symmetry spectrum (Li 2025a, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Li2025Low](#ref-Li2025Low)], excludes other unstable eigenvalues, and deduces asymptotic stability with an $H^1$-open blowup set in dimensions one through ten (Li 2025b, Theorems 1.2–1.3) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Li2025High](#ref-Li2025High)]. Our outgoing and multiplicity arguments treat the defocusing large-power profile in dimension twelve.

The remaining analysis separates a local compact error from global dissipation. Section <a href="#sec:linear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:linear">5</a> uses this estimate to transfer decay from the whole-space evolution to expanding tori, retaining the norm that might escape in a merely local limit. Finally, Section <a href="#sec:nonlinear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:nonlinear">6</a> constructs a graph of decaying perturbations and varies the physical blowup parameters. A degree argument turns that graph into an open set of physical data. The Gaussian support argument then proves Corollary <a href="#cor:gaussian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:gaussian">2</a>.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Notation and choices of parameters.

Throughout the construction $d=12$, $a>0$ is small, and $D=y\cdot\nabla$. The integer $p=1+1/a$ is restricted to odd values when polynomial smoothness is used. After choosing an admissible profile and fixing $a$, we choose a sufficiently large finite integer $k$. Later choices of a time-step length, a torus scale, and a data neighborhood are made in that order. Implicit constants can depend on previously fixed parameters. When a limit as $a\to0$ is used, the required uniformities are stated explicitly. Complex-valued evolution spaces are regarded as real spaces for the linearization, and are complexified only for spectral analysis.

<a id="sec:free"></a>
### Free exterior matching

The profile construction and the spectral count both use a free radial equation in dimension twelve. We study it first as an independent matching problem. For a real parameter $b$, a spectral parameter $\lambda\in\mathbb C$, an angular degree $\ell\in\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$, and a channel index $h=\pm1$, the equation is <a id="free:radial-equation"></a>$$\label{free:radial-equation}
 v_h''+\left(\frac{11}{r}+\frac{hi r}{2}\right)v_h'
 +\left(b+hi\lambda-\frac{\ell(\ell+10)}{r^2}\right)v_h=0.$$ Here $\ell(\ell+10)$ is the eigenvalue of the negative spherical Laplacian on degree-$\ell$ harmonics on $\mathbb S^{11}$. The substitution $v_h=r^\ell H(q,6+\ell,x)$, with $q=\lambda+\ell/2-hib$ and $x=-hi r^2/4$, gives the confluent hypergeometric equation. Its slow solution is $$r^\ell H(\lambda+\ell/2-hi b,6+\ell,-hi r^2/4),$$ where Lemma <a href="#free:slow" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:slow">4</a> defines $H$ by its integral and its nonoscillatory asymptotic expansion. Primes on $v_h$ denote radial derivatives; primes on $H$ denote derivatives in its last argument. We need two kinds of boundary data from this solution. At $\lambda=\ell=0$ in channel $h=1$, the quotient $j=-H'/H$ vanishes exactly when the radial derivative vanishes; this matches the exterior to a constant core. For general $\lambda,\ell$, the two value-and-derivative columns in Proposition <a href="#free:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:main">3</a> define the matching determinant whose zeros we count.

Section <a href="#sec:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:profiles">3</a> derives the similarity equation and constructs the finite-power profiles. In those variables $b$ is the logarithmic phase frequency and a spectral mode has time factor $e^{\lambda s}$, where $s$ is logarithmic similarity time. Lemma <a href="#spec:interface" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:interface">30</a> will identify the determinant studied here with the limiting constrained spectral problem. The calculations in this section concern its free exterior, where the power parameter is $a=0$ and the pressure is zero.

<a id="free:disk"></a>
All terminating decimals in this section denote exact rational numbers. Set $$\label{free:disk}
 \begin{gathered}
 b_*=.33477606871236,\qquad Z_*=2.70506819293654,\qquad
 \delta=10^{-8},\\
 \mathcal D=\{(b,Z)\in\mathbb R^2:(b-b_*)^2+(Z-Z_*)^2\le\delta^2\}.
 \end{gathered}$$ The radius of the limiting core is $R=2\sqrt Z$. We prove the following statement, including its finite arithmetic ingredients. The definition of $H$ follows immediately below. The degree on $\mathcal D$ means Brouwer degree on its interior.

<a id="stmt-free-main"></a>
<div id="free:main" class="proposition">

**Proposition 3** (Free matching). *For every $(b,Z)\in\mathcal D$, the following conclusions hold.*

1.  *The quotient $$j(b,Z)=-\frac{H'(-ib,6,-iZ)}{H(-ib,6,-iZ)}$$ is well defined and satisfies $|j(b,Z)|<3\cdot10^{-9}$. It is nonzero on $\partial\mathcal D$, and, identifying $\mathbb C$ with $\mathbb R^2$ in the order real part, imaginary part, $$\deg(j,\mathcal D,0)=-1.$$*

<a id="free:exterior-bounds"></a>
2.  *For every real $z\ge Z$, $H(-ib,6,-iz)\ne0$ and $$\label{free:exterior-bounds}
     \operatorname{Re}j(b,z)<.043.$$ For $z\ge3$ one also has $\operatorname{Im}j(b,z)>0$.*

3.  *For $\ell\in\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$ and $h\in\{1,-1\}$ define <a id="free:jet"></a>$$\label{free:jet}
     q_h=\lambda+\ell/2-hi b,\qquad
     J_h(\lambda)=
     \begin{pmatrix}H(q_h,6+\ell,-hiZ)\\-H'(q_h,6+\ell,-hiZ)\end{pmatrix}.$$ The columns $J_h$ are holomorphic and nonzero on a neighborhood of $\operatorname{Re}\lambda\ge-1/32$. The determinant $\Delta_\ell(\lambda;b,Z)=\det(J_+(\lambda),J_-(\lambda))$ has no zero on $\operatorname{Re}\lambda=-1/32$. In the closed half-plane $\operatorname{Re}\lambda\ge-1/32$ its numbers of zeros, counted with holomorphic multiplicity, are $$\begin{array}{c|ccc}
     \ell&0&1&\ell\ge2\\ \hline
     \#\{\Delta_\ell=0\}&2&1&0.
     \end{array}$$*

*The locations of the symmetry zeros are identified later at parameters where $j(b,Z)=0$; the count in this proposition holds on the entire disk.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The slow solution and the Laguerre cone

The function $H(q,m,x)$ below is Tricomi’s confluent hypergeometric function $U(q,m,x)$; see (National Institute of Standards and Technology, n.d., Equations 13.4.4 and 13.3.22). We prove the regularized representation and the uniform symbol bounds needed on the imaginary rays.

<a id="stmt-free-slow"></a>
<div id="free:slow" class="lemma">

<a id="free:H-definition"></a>
<a id="free:H-equation"></a>
<a id="free:H-expansion"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Regularized slow solution). *For $\operatorname{Re}q>-1$, $\operatorname{Re}x\ge0$, $x\ne0$, define, with principal powers, $$\label{free:H-definition}
 H(q,m,x)=x^{-q}\left\{1+\frac1{\Gamma(q)}
 \int_0^\infty e^{-u}u^{q-1}
 \left[(1+u/x)^{m-1-q}-1\right]\,du\right\}.$$ Here $m$ may in particular be any positive integer used below. This definition is holomorphic in $q$, including $q=0$, and in $x$ on the interior of the indicated region, with holomorphic continuation across each nonzero point of its imaginary boundary. Primes on $H$ denote derivatives in its last argument. It satisfies $$\label{free:H-equation}
 xH''+(m-x)H'-qH=0,\qquad
 H'(q,m,x)=-qH(q+1,m+1,x),\qquad H(0,m,x)=1.$$ For every $N\ge1$ it has the expansion $$\label{free:H-expansion}
 H(q,m,x)=x^{-q}\left\{
 \sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{(-1)^j(q)_j(1+q-m)_j}{j!x^j}
 +O(|x|^{-N})\right\}.$$ Here $(q)_0=1$ and $(q)_j=\prod_{n=0}^{j-1}(q+n)$ for $j\ge1$. The expansion is uniform for $\operatorname{Re}x\ge0$, $|x|\ge1$, and compact parameter sets. The same remainder estimate holds after any fixed number of $x\partial_x$ derivatives of the bracket. The symbol expansion specifies the slow solution, without an oscillatory component on the imaginary rays.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-free-slow"><summary>Proof of stmt-free-slow</summary>

*Proof.* The bracket in the integrand of <a href="#free:H-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:H-definition">[free:H-definition]</a> is $O(u)$ at zero, locally uniformly in the parameters; at infinity it and its parameter derivatives grow at most polynomially, with additional powers of $\log u$. Thus the regularized integral is locally holomorphic for $\operatorname{Re}q>-1$. The reciprocal gamma function vanishes at zero, which proves the last assertion in <a href="#free:H-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:H-equation">[free:H-equation]</a>.

For $\operatorname{Re}q>0$ the expression in braces can be combined into the usual integral. On $x>0$, substitution $u=xv$ gives $$H(q,m,x)=\frac1{\Gamma(q)}\int_0^\infty
 e^{-xv}v^{q-1}(1+v)^{m-1-q}\,dv.$$ Differentiation and integration by parts prove both the equation and the derivative identity; holomorphic continuation proves them on the stated domain. The branches in the regularized integral are regular on a neighborhood of each nonzero imaginary $x$, so the boundary values used in the matching problem are genuine holomorphic values.

To obtain <a href="#free:H-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:H-expansion">[free:H-expansion]</a>, split the integral at $u=|x|/2$. Taylor’s formula applies uniformly to $(1+u/x)^{m-1-q}$ on the first piece. Its $j$th coefficient integrates to $(-1)^j(q)_j(1+q-m)_j/j!$. On the second piece the exponential weight bounds the integral by any prescribed inverse power of $|x|$; the same argument applies after symbol differentiation. This proves the full assertion about remainders. For completeness, the other local solution at infinity obtained by reduction of order has the form $e^xx^{q-m}$ times an inverse-power expansion. On either imaginary ray its repeated symbol derivatives acquire arbitrarily high powers of $|x|$. It therefore cannot be added to <a href="#free:H-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:H-expansion">[free:H-expansion]</a> while preserving all its symbol estimates. This also explains the slow normalization on the rays. ◻

</details>

Fix $h,\ell,b,Z$ and write $$M=\ell+5,\quad s=hiZ,\quad q=\lambda+\ell/2-hi b,
 \quad G(t)=H(q,M+1,t-s)\quad(t\ge0).$$ All parameter analyticity statements below concern $\lambda$; estimates are locally uniform in the other displayed parameters.

<a id="stmt-free-laguerre"></a>
<div id="free:laguerre" class="lemma">

<a id="free:initial-tail"></a>
<a id="free:cone-form"></a>
<a id="free:cone-increment"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Laguerre recurrence). *Let $L_n(t)=e^t\partial_t^n(e^{-t}t^n)/n!$ be the Laguerre polynomials, orthonormal for $e^{-t}\,dt$ on $[0,\infty)$. Write $$G=\sum_{n\ge0}g_nL_n,\qquad
 B_n=\sum_{k\ge n}g_k,\qquad C_n=\sum_{k\ge n}(k-n)g_k.$$ These series and their parameter derivatives converge rapidly. With $t_n=q+n$ and $d_n=t_n-M$, one has <a id="free:recurrence"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 &(B_0,C_0)=(G(0),-G'(0)),\label{free:initial-tail}\\
 &t_ng_n=-MB_{n+1}-sC_n,\qquad
 d_nB_{n+1}=t_nB_n+sC_n,\qquad
 C_{n+1}=C_n-B_{n+1}.\label{free:recurrence}
\end{aligned}$$ The real quadratic form $$\label{free:cone-form}
 p_n=\frac M2|B_n|^2+\operatorname{Re}(s\overline{B_n}C_n)$$ satisfies the exact identity $$\label{free:cone-increment}
 p_{n+1}-p_n=
 (\operatorname{Re}\lambda+n-5/2)|g_n|^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-free-laguerre"><summary>Proof of stmt-free-laguerre</summary>

*Proof.* Integration by parts gives orthonormality and $[t\partial_t^2+(1-t)\partial_t]L_n=-nL_n$. Polynomials are complete: a function orthogonal to all powers has a Laplace transform whose derivatives vanish at an interior point of its domain of holomorphy, hence its Laplace transform, and then the function, vanishes. The function $G$ and all its derivatives are smooth at zero and of polynomial growth at infinity by Lemma <a href="#free:slow" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:slow">4</a>. Repeated integration by parts with this Laguerre operator shows that $g_n$, and the Laguerre coefficients of each derivative of $G$, decrease faster than every inverse power of $n$. The boundary terms vanish because the coefficient of the derivative at zero is $t$, and the weight at infinity is exponential. These arguments are uniform on compact parameter sets, also after holomorphic differentiation.

The generating function gives, initially for $0\le x<1$, $$\sum_{n\ge0}g_nx^n=
 \int_0^\infty e^{-u}G((1-x)u)\,du.$$ Taking its value and first derivative as $x\uparrow1$ proves <a href="#free:initial-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:initial-tail">[free:initial-tail]</a>. Since $L_k'=-\sum_{n<k}L_n$, the coefficients of $G'$ are $-B_{n+1}$, whereas those of $G''-G'$ are $C_n$. Rewriting the differential equation as $$[t\partial_t^2+(1-t)\partial_t]G+MG'-s(G''-G')-qG=0$$ proves the first recurrence. The other two follow from $g_n=B_n-B_{n+1}$ and the definition of $C_n$.

Finally insert $B_{n+1}=B_n-g_n$ and $C_{n+1}=C_n-B_{n+1}$ into <a href="#free:cone-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:cone-form">[free:cone-form]</a>. Since $s$ is purely imaginary, the cross term $\operatorname{Re}(s|B_{n+1}|^2)$ vanishes. Use $t_ng_n=-MB_{n+1}-sC_n$ to simplify the remaining terms. The coefficient of $|g_n|^2$ becomes $\operatorname{Re}t_n-M/2=\operatorname{Re}\lambda+n-5/2$, which is <a href="#free:cone-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:cone-increment">[free:cone-increment]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-free-cone"></a>
<div id="free:cone" class="lemma">

<a id="free:strict-cone"></a>
<a id="free:tail-disk"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Strict normalized cone). *Suppose $\operatorname{Re}\lambda\ge-1/32$ and $K\ge3$. For $n\ge K$ the normalized coefficients $\widehat g_n=g_n/(q)_K$, and their sums $\widehat B_K=B_K/(q)_K$, $\widehat C_K=C_K/(q)_K$, extend holomorphically across $q=0$. They satisfy $$\label{free:strict-cone}
 \frac M2|\widehat B_K|^2+
 \operatorname{Re}(s\overline{\widehat B_K}\widehat C_K)
 =-\sum_{n\ge K}(\operatorname{Re}\lambda+n-5/2)
 |\widehat g_n|^2<0.$$ Consequently $\widehat C_K\ne0$, and $$\label{free:tail-disk}
 R_h=\widehat B_K/\widehat C_K\quad\hbox{is holomorphic},\qquad
 |R_h+s/M|<Z/M.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-free-cone"><summary>Proof of stmt-free-cone</summary>

*Proof.* The only zero of $(q)_K$ in the specified half-plane is $q=0$, and it is simple. It can occur only for $\ell=0$. At this point $G=1$, so every $g_n$ with $n\ge1$ vanishes. Holomorphic division therefore removes the apparent singularity for $n\ge K$. Local Cauchy estimates preserve the rapid decay established above, justifying division of the sums and continuation of their identities.

<a id="free:normalized-derivative"></a>
The normalized tail cannot vanish identically in its sequence index. Indeed repeated use of <a href="#free:H-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:H-equation">[free:H-equation]</a> gives $$\label{free:normalized-derivative}
 \frac{\partial_t^K G}{(q)_K}
 =(-1)^KH(q+K,M+1+K,t-s).$$ The right side is nonzero by its leading asymptotic coefficient, also at $q=0$. If all normalized coefficients with $n\ge K$ vanished, the function $[G-\sum_{n<K}g_nL_n]/(q)_K$, with its removable value at zero, would vanish. Its $K$th derivative would be zero, contradicting <a href="#free:normalized-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:normalized-derivative">[free:normalized-derivative]</a>.

Sum <a href="#free:cone-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:cone-increment">[free:cone-increment]</a> from $K$ to infinity and divide by $|(q)_K|^2$, taking the removable value when necessary. Rapid decay makes the limiting form zero. Each weight in the resulting sum is positive because $K\ge3$ and $\operatorname{Re}\lambda\ge-1/32$. The preceding nontriviality gives strictness in <a href="#free:strict-cone" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:strict-cone">[free:strict-cone]</a>. If $\widehat C_K$ were zero the left side would be nonnegative. Dividing by its nonzero squared modulus and completing the square proves <a href="#free:tail-disk" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:tail-disk">[free:tail-disk]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-free-columns"></a>
<div id="free:columns" class="lemma">

<a id="free:backward"></a>
<a id="free:column-identity"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Analytic matching columns). *Define $$\label{free:backward}
 F_n=\begin{pmatrix}d_n-s&-s\\t_n&t_n\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 P_K=F_0F_1\cdots F_{K-1}.$$ Then, including parameters where an individual $t_n$ or $d_n$ vanishes, $$\label{free:column-identity}
 P_K\begin{pmatrix}R_h\\1\end{pmatrix}
 =\frac1{\widehat C_K}
 \begin{pmatrix}H(q,M+1,-s)\\-H'(q,M+1,-s)\end{pmatrix}.$$ The scalar on the right is holomorphic and nonzero. The column is nonzero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-free-columns"><summary>Proof of stmt-free-columns</summary>

*Proof.* The recurrences imply the division-free identity $F_n(B_{n+1},C_{n+1})^t=t_n(B_n,C_n)^t$. Multiplying gives $P_K(B_K,C_K)^t=(q)_K(B_0,C_0)^t$. Divide by $(q)_K$ and use Lemma <a href="#free:cone" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:cone">6</a>; analytic continuation gives the identity at the removable point. A simultaneous zero of $H$ and $H'$ at $x=-s\ne0$ would, by uniqueness for its second order equation, make $H$ identically zero, contrary to <a href="#free:H-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:H-expansion">[free:H-expansion]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Exclusion of high angular degrees

<a id="stmt-free-high-angular"></a>
<div id="free:high-angular" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8**. *For $(b,Z)\in\mathcal D$, $\ell\ge4$, and $\operatorname{Re}\lambda\ge-1/32$, one has $\Delta_\ell(\lambda;b,Z)\ne0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-free-high-angular"><summary>Proof of stmt-free-high-angular</summary>

<a id="free:gauged"></a>
*Proof.* Suppose the determinant vanished. Since the two columns are nonzero, multiply their functions by nonzero constants to arrange $H_+(-iZ)=H_-(iZ)$ and $H_+'(-iZ)=H_-'(iZ)$. Here $H_h(x)$ denotes the resulting multiple of $H(\lambda+\ell/2-hi b,M+1,x)$ and $M=\ell+5\ge9$. The substitution $U_h=e^{-x/2}x^{M/2}H_h$ gives $$\label{free:gauged}
 -(x\partial_xU_h)_x+\left(\frac x4+\frac{M^2}{4x}\right)U_h
 =(3-\lambda+hi b)U_h.$$

Integrate this equation along the conjugate paths $$\begin{gathered}
 x_h(\eta)=u-hi v,\quad x_h(0)=-hiZ,\quad
 g_h=x_h'=e^{-hi\vartheta},\\
 \vartheta=\min(2\eta,\vartheta_0),\quad
 \cos\vartheta_0=\frac{99}{101},\quad
 \sin\vartheta_0=\frac{20}{101}.
 \end{gathered}$$ Write $\eta_0=\vartheta_0/2$ and $$x_h/g_h^2=L+hiN,\qquad
 L=u\cos(2\vartheta)+v\sin(2\vartheta),\quad
 N=u\sin(2\vartheta)-v\cos(2\vartheta).$$ In particular $L>0$ for $\eta>0$ and $$L'=\cos\vartheta-2\vartheta'N,\qquad
 N'=\sin\vartheta+2\vartheta'L.$$ The real part of $x$ tends to $+\infty$ linearly, so the exponential factor in $U_h$ justifies every boundary limit and integration below.

<a id="free:multiplier-identity"></a>
<a id="free:multiplier-potential"></a>
For a bounded real function $\alpha$, smooth on each side of $\eta_0$, put $B_c=\alpha L+\vartheta'N/2$. Pair <a href="#free:gauged" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:gauged">[free:gauged]</a> with $\overline U_h\,d\eta$ and take real parts. The kinetic expression after integration by parts is $$L|U_h'|^2+\operatorname{Re}
 \big(hi\vartheta'(L+hiN)\overline U_hU_h'\big).$$ Completing the square writes this as $L|U_h'+(\alpha-hi\vartheta'/2)U_h|^2
-B_c(|U_h|^2)'-L(\alpha^2+\vartheta'^2/4)|U_h|^2$. The real lower endpoint terms before this completion sum to $\sum_h(M/2)|U_h(0)|^2$. To see the cancellation, expand $x\overline U_h\partial_xU_h$ using $U_h=e^{-x/2}x^{M/2}H_h$: the terms containing $x\overline H_hH_h'$ cancel between the two matched jets, and the real contribution from the gauge is $M/2$. Since $B_c(0)=-Z$, integration of the displayed derivative, separately on the two path pieces, now yields $$\begin{aligned}
\label{free:multiplier-identity}
 0=\sum_{h=\pm1}\Bigg\{&
 (M/2-Z)|U_h(0)|^2+
 [B_c(\eta_0+)-B_c(\eta_0-)]|U_h(\eta_0)|^2\notag\\
 &+\int_0^\infty
 \left[L|U_h'+(\alpha-hi\vartheta'/2)U_h|^2
       +V_c|U_h|^2\right]\,d\eta\Bigg\},
\end{aligned}$$ where, with $\sigma=\operatorname{Re}\lambda$, $$\label{free:multiplier-potential}
 V_c=\sigma-3+\frac u4+\frac{M^2u}{4(u^2+v^2)}
 +\frac{\vartheta'\sin\vartheta}{2}
 +\frac34L\vartheta'^2+(\alpha L)'-L\alpha^2.$$ There is no missing corner term: it is precisely the jump displayed in <a href="#free:multiplier-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:multiplier-identity">[free:multiplier-identity]</a>.

<a id="free:high-polynomial"></a>
On the arc take $\alpha=.3$. Here $u=\sin\vartheta/2$, $v=Z+(1-\cos\vartheta)/2$, and $$u\le.1,\qquad 2.70<v<2.72,\qquad
 N\le.04-2.70\cdot.92=-2.444,
 \qquad L'>10.7.$$ It follows directly from <a href="#free:multiplier-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:multiplier-potential">[free:multiplier-potential]</a>, dropping its nonnegative $u$ terms and sine term, that $V_c>-3-1/32+.3(10.7)+(3-.09)L>0$. At the join $1.13<L(\eta_0)<1.19$. On the straight piece, with $t=\eta-\eta_0$, choose $$\alpha L=-1.5+\frac{5t}{1+1.5t}.$$ The jump is $-1.5-.3L(\eta_0)-N(\eta_0)>0$. The geometry of the ray gives $$u_0=.099+.98t\le u\le.100+.981t,\qquad
 v\le2.72+.20t,\qquad L\ge L_0=1.13+.98t.$$ Set $d_0=(.100+.981t)^2+(2.72+.20t)^2$ and $q_0=1+1.5t$. Since $M^2\ge81$, $V_cL_0d_0q_0^2$ is bounded below by $$\begin{aligned}
\label{free:high-polynomial}
 P(t)={}&(-3-1/32+u_0/4)L_0d_0q_0^2
 +\frac{81}{4}u_0L_0q_0^2+5L_0d_0
 -(-1.5+2.75t)^2d_0\notag\\
 >&2+t(33-72.2t+44.8t^2)
       +t^4(17.9-4.61t+.54t^2)>0.
\end{aligned}$$ The first strict comparison is coefficientwise exact arithmetic; the last two quadratic discriminants are $-17519/25$ and $-174119/10000$. Appendix <a href="#cert:high-angular" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:high-angular">7.4</a> provides the full polynomial recipe and verifier. Thus all terms of <a href="#free:multiplier-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:multiplier-identity">[free:multiplier-identity]</a> are nonnegative, the bulk potential is strictly positive, and $M/2>Z$. It follows that $U_h=0$, a contradiction. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Boundary homotopy and the low angular counts

We use $K=8$ for $0\le\ell\le3$. For $0\le\rho\le1$ replace the tail ratio in <a href="#free:column-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:column-identity">[free:column-identity]</a> by $\rho R_h$. The closure of the disk in <a href="#free:tail-disk" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:tail-disk">[free:tail-disk]</a> is convex and contains zero, so every terminal pair $(\rho R_h,1)$ satisfies $p_K\le0$. The resulting columns are holomorphic functions of $\lambda$, continuous in $\rho$. We must exclude determinant zeros on a common counting contour.

<a id="stmt-free-boundary"></a>
<div id="free:boundary" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Counting boundary). *There is a rectangle with left edge $\operatorname{Re}\lambda=-1/32$ and other edges sufficiently far to the right and above and below, uniformly for $(b,Z)\in\mathcal D$ and $0\le\rho\le1$, on whose boundary the homotoped determinant is nonzero. The same exclusions hold on every larger such rectangle.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-free-boundary"><summary>Proof of stmt-free-boundary</summary>

<a id="free:summed-increment"></a>
*Proof.* Choose the edges so that $t_n,d_n\ne0$ for $n<8$ on them. If the columns became dependent at such a point, their nonzero common initial jet $(B_0,C_0)$ would have two forward images satisfying $p_{8,h}\le0$. Indeed the backward matrices are invertible there and their inverses are exactly the forward recurrences, up to nonzero scalar factors. For the same initial jet in both channels, $$\label{free:summed-increment}
 p_{8,+}+p_{8,-}=M|B_0|^2+
 \sum_{h=\pm1}\sum_{n=0}^7
 (\sigma+n-5/2)|g_{n,h}|^2.$$ For $\sigma>5/2$ all increment weights are positive. If $B_0\ne0$ the first term is positive; if $B_0=0$ and $C_0\ne0$, then $g_{0,h}=-s_hC_0/d_{0,h}\ne0$. This excludes the right edge, which can be taken beyond every possible zero of its divisors.

For the horizontal edges let $v=\operatorname{Im}\lambda$, $C_0=vc$, and normalize $|B_0|^2+|c|^2=1$. Uniformly for $\sigma$ in a bounded interval and $n<8$, $$g_{0,h}=-(MB_0+s_hC_0)/d_{0,h}=-hZc+o(1),\qquad
 g_{n+1,h}=\frac{t_{n,h}g_{n,h}+s_hB_{n+1,h}}{d_{n+1,h}}
             =g_{n,h}+o(1).$$ Consequently <a href="#free:summed-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:summed-increment">[free:summed-increment]</a> equals $$M|B_0|^2+2(8\sigma+8)Z^2|c|^2+o(1)>0$$ for large $|v|$, since $8\sigma+8\ge31/4$. This proves the two horizontal exclusions uniformly.

<a id="free:forward-matrix"></a>
<a id="free:boundary-form"></a>
For the left edge set $s=iZ$ and form the forward matrix without its divisors: $$\label{free:forward-matrix}
 T=T_{K-1}\cdots T_0,\qquad
 T_n=\begin{pmatrix}t_n&s\\-t_n&d_n-s\end{pmatrix},\qquad K=8.$$ Thus the actual forward jet is $T(B_0,C_0)^t/\prod_{n<K}d_n$. Define $$\label{free:boundary-form}
 \mathsf H(v)=T^*
 \begin{pmatrix}M&s\\-s&0\end{pmatrix}T,
 \qquad \mathsf S(v)=\mathsf H(v)+\overline{\mathsf H(-v)}.$$ The second summand is the form for $h=-1$. Each summand evaluates to $2|\prod d_{n,h}|^2p_{K,h}$ on the common initial pair. We shall prove $\mathsf S(v)>0$ for every real $v$, excluding the two simultaneous nonpositive values.

First, its determinant is a real even polynomial of degree at most $4K-2=30$ for every real $(b,Z)$ in the parameter box. Hermitian symmetry gives real diagonal entries, and the reflection in <a href="#free:boundary-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:boundary-form">[free:boundary-form]</a> makes the diagonal entries even and the off-diagonal entry satisfy $C(-v)=\overline{C(v)}$. To check the degree bound structurally, hold the initial jet fixed and let $|v|\to\infty$. The forward recurrences give $g_{n,h}=O((|B_0|+|C_0|)/|v|)$ for $n<K$. Also $|\prod d_{n,h}|^2=|v|^{2K}(1+O(1/|v|))$. Using <a href="#free:cone-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:cone-increment">[free:cone-increment]</a>, each form divided by $|v|^{2K}$ has its initial cone form as leading term and an $O(v^{-2})$ increment. The opposite initial cross terms cancel in their sum. Thus the first diagonal, off-diagonal, and second diagonal entries of $\mathsf S/|v|^{2K}$ have respective orders $O(1)$, $O(v^{-1})$, $O(v^{-2})$. This proves the degree bound, independently of any cancellation at a central parameter value. Its first diagonal is positive for sufficiently large $|v|$.

The exact coefficient calculation in Appendix <a href="#cert:boundary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:boundary">7.2</a> proves $\det\mathsf S(v)>0$ for all real $v$ and all parameters in $\mathcal D$. A continuous Hermitian matrix with strictly positive determinant cannot change its signature. Its positive first diagonal at large $|v|$ therefore implies $\mathsf S(v)>0$ on the whole line. This completes the left-edge exclusion and the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-free-winding"></a>
<div id="free:winding" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Winding count). *For $0\le\ell\le3$ the zero counts in Proposition <a href="#free:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:main">3</a> are $2,1,0,0$, respectively.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-free-winding"><summary>Proof of stmt-free-winding</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#free:boundary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:boundary">9</a> and the argument principle preserve total zero multiplicity throughout $\rho:1\to0$. At $\rho=1$, Lemma <a href="#free:columns" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:columns">7</a> identifies this count with the true matching determinant by nonzero holomorphic scalar factors. Individual homotoped columns may vanish at interior singular factors of $P_K$; these are zeros of a holomorphic determinant and do not affect the boundary argument. No assertion of interior nonvanishing is needed.

We may also move $(b,Z)$ to $(B/H_0,S/H_0)$, where $H_0=10^8$, $B=33477607$, $S=270506819$. This point lies in $\mathcal D$, and the preceding exclusions hold on the connecting segment. At zero tail ratio, put $\lambda=-1/32+X$ and let $(\widetilde x,\widetilde y)^t=P_K(0,1)^t$ in channel $h=1$. The real polynomial $$P_\ell(X)=\operatorname{Im}_{\mathrm{coeff}}
 (\widetilde x(X)\overline{\widetilde y}(X))$$ is a nonzero constant multiple of the determinant; the bar here conjugates coefficients. Induction in the backward recurrence shows that its degree is $15=2K-1$, with unscaled leading coefficient $-KZ$: $\widetilde y$ has leading term $X^K$ and $\widetilde x$ has leading term $-KiZ X^{K-1}$. Write <a id="free:axis-polynomials"></a>$$\label{free:axis-polynomials}
 P_\ell(iv)=\mathcal R_\ell(v^2)+iv\mathcal I_\ell(v^2).$$ Both real polynomials have degree at most seven.

The rational signs in Appendix <a href="#cert:winding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:winding">7.3</a> give the following root counts; all roots in the table are simple: $$\begin{array}{c|cc|cc}
 &\multicolumn{2}{c|}{\text{positive roots}}&
   \multicolumn{2}{c}{\text{negative roots}}\\
 \ell&\mathcal R_\ell&\mathcal I_\ell&\mathcal R_\ell&\mathcal I_\ell\\\hline
 0&5&6&2&1\\
 1&6&6&1&1\\
 2,3&7&7&0&0
\end{array}$$ Here is why signs alone suffice. Successive positive separators have alternating one-coordinate sign changes, following the quadrant cycle $++,-+,--,+-$. They therefore give the listed distinct positive roots, interlaced between the two polynomials. For $\ell=0,1$ the additional values at $-1/4,-4$ supply the listed negative roots. These distinct roots exhaust each degree bound of seven. Hence there are no further roots, none repeated, and no unrecorded axis crossings.

The curve $P_\ell(iv)$ for $v>0$ crosses each successive axis in the positive rotational direction and finishes in the first quadrant. Let $n=5,6,7$ be the positive root count of $\mathcal R_\ell$. Starting with the real value at zero and using the final odd-degree asymptotic, the continuous argument change on $[0,\infty)$ is $(n+1/2)\pi$. This also follows by counting the consecutive quadrants from the initial signs $-+$, $++$, $--$ in the three rows. Conjugation doubles that change on the full oriented imaginary axis. A single factor $iv-z$ contributes $+\pi$ if $\operatorname{Re}z<0$ and $-\pi$ if $\operatorname{Re}z>0$. Thus $$N_{\mathrm{left}}-N_{\mathrm{right}}=2n+1,
 \qquad N_{\mathrm{left}}+N_{\mathrm{right}}=15,$$ and $N_{\mathrm{right}}=(15-(2n+1))/2=2,1,0$. The counting boundary has no zeros, so these are also the counts in the closed half-plane. The count transfers back by the homotopies. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Profile degree and nonvanishing of the exterior

<a id="stmt-free-profile-degree"></a>
<div id="free:profile-degree" class="lemma">

<a id="free:profile-linearization"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Profile matching degree). *The first conclusion of Proposition <a href="#free:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:main">3</a> holds. More precisely there is an invertible real-linear map $\mathcal L:\mathbb R^2\to\mathbb C$ with negative determinant and minimum stretch greater than $.048$ such that $$\label{free:profile-linearization}
 |j(b,Z)-\mathcal L(b-b_*,Z-Z_*)|<2.5\cdot10^{-11}
 \qquad((b,Z)\in\mathcal D).$$ In particular $|j|>4.55\cdot10^{-10}$ on $\partial\mathcal D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-free-profile-degree"><summary>Proof of stmt-free-profile-degree</summary>

<a id="free:profile-enclosures"></a>
<a id="free:denominator-gap"></a>
*Proof.* Use $h=1$, $\ell=\lambda=0$, $K=34$ in <a href="#free:backward" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:backward">[free:backward]</a>, dividing its $n$th factor by $n+1$, and call the resulting product $L(b,Z)$. Set $$U_0=L(b_*,Z_*),\quad X_0=\partial_bL(b_*,Z_*),\quad
 Y_0=\partial_ZL(b_*,Z_*),\quad m_0=(U_0)_{01}.$$ All matrix indices here are zero-based. The exact rational enclosures of Appendix <a href="#cert:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:profile">7.5</a> give $$\begin{gathered}
 9<|m_0|<10,\qquad
 (U_0)_{00}/m_0\in(2.835,2.837)+i(1.426,1.428),\notag\\
 |(U_0)_{11}/m_0|<10^{-12},\qquad
 (X_0)_{11}/m_0\in(-.0811,-.0810)+i(.1361,.1363),\notag\\
 (Y_0)_{11}/m_0\in(-.0001,.0001)+i(.1237,.1240),\notag\\
 \max_{i,j}|(X_0)_{ij}/m_0|,
 \max_{i,j}|(Y_0)_{ij}/m_0|<20,\notag\\
 \max_{i,j}|L_{ij}-(U_0)_{ij}-(b-b_*)(X_0)_{ij}
                   -(Z-Z_*)(Y_0)_{ij}|<50000|m_0|\delta^2,\notag\\
 |\det L|<6.2\cdot10^{-12}|m_0|^2.\label{free:profile-enclosures}
\end{gathered}$$ The same certificate, propagating these estimates by rational inequalities, gives $$\label{free:denominator-gap}
 |L_{01}-iZL_{00}/5|-Z|L_{00}|/5>.6|m_0|,
 \qquad |L_{01}/m_0-1|<5\cdot10^{-7}.$$ Since the tail ratio lies in the disk with center $-iZ/5$ and radius $Z/5$, the first component $L_{00}R_++L_{01}$ cannot vanish. Lemma <a href="#free:columns" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:columns">7</a> proves the desired nonvanishing of $H$ before we take its logarithmic derivative.

The second-to-first component ratio is $j(b,Z)$. Subtracting its value at zero tail gives the exact identity $$j-\frac{L_{11}}{L_{01}}
 =-\frac{R_+\det L}{L_{01}(L_{00}R_++L_{01})}.$$ Using $|R_+|\le2Z/5$ and <a href="#free:denominator-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:denominator-gap">[free:denominator-gap]</a> bounds its modulus by $$\frac{(2Z/5)|\det L|}{.6|m_0|\,|L_{01}|}.$$ Define $$\mathcal L(\eta,\zeta)=
 \eta\frac{(X_0)_{11}}{m_0}+\zeta\frac{(Y_0)_{11}}{m_0}.$$ Appendix <a href="#cert:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:profile">7.5</a> gives the exact propagation of the displayed bounds to <a href="#free:profile-linearization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:profile-linearization">[free:profile-linearization]</a>, the minimum stretch bound, and $|\mathcal L(\eta,\zeta)|\le.283\delta$ on the disk. Its determinant is negative already from the two enclosing rectangles. The boundary size of the linear map exceeds $.048\delta=4.8\cdot10^{-10}$; subtracting the error proves the stated boundary margin. Straight homotopy to $\mathcal L$ avoids zero on the boundary, so its degree is $-1$. Finally $.283\delta+2.5\cdot10^{-11}<3\cdot10^{-9}$ proves the disk bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-free-exterior"></a>
<div id="free:exterior" class="lemma">

<a id="free:radial-quotients"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Exterior inequalities). *For $(b,Z)\in\mathcal D$, $H(-ib,6,-iz)$ is nonzero for all $z\ge Z$, and the inequalities in <a href="#free:exterior-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:exterior-bounds">[free:exterior-bounds]</a> and $\operatorname{Im}j(b,z)>0$ for $z\ge3$ hold. In radial variables, for any nonzero constant multiple $Q(r)$ of $H(-ib,6,-ir^2/4)$, $$\label{free:radial-quotients}
 \frac{A'}A=-\frac r2\operatorname{Im}j(b,r^2/4),\qquad
 \frac wr=\frac12+\operatorname{Re}j(b,r^2/4),
 \quad A=|Q|,\quad w=r/2+2\partial_r\arg Q.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-free-exterior"><summary>Proof of stmt-free-exterior</summary>

<a id="free:exterior-forms"></a>
*Proof.* Use the forward matrix <a href="#free:forward-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:forward-matrix">[free:forward-matrix]</a> at $\lambda=\ell=0$, $s=iz$, $K=5$, and let $$\mathsf H=T^*\begin{pmatrix}5&iz\\-iz&0\end{pmatrix}T
   =\begin{pmatrix}A_0&\overline c\\c&d_0\end{pmatrix}.$$ The exact coefficient checks in Appendix <a href="#cert:exterior" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:exterior">7.6</a> prove $$\begin{aligned}
\label{free:exterior-forms}
 &d_0>0,\qquad .043d_0+\operatorname{Re}c-1130z>0
 &&(z\ge2.704),\notag\\
 &-\operatorname{Im}c-1130z>0&&(z\ge3),\notag\\
 &|\det T|=\prod_{n=0}^4|t_nd_n|<1130.
\end{aligned}$$ Every $b$ in the disk is positive, so all forward divisors are nonzero. The exact jet has nonpositive terminal cone form, hence $J^*\mathsf HJ\le0$. If its first component were zero, positivity of $d_0$ would force its second component to be zero, contradicting Lemma <a href="#free:columns" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:columns">7</a>. Thus $j$ is well defined. Moreover $$\det\mathsf H=-z^2|\det T|^2,
 \qquad \left|j+\frac c{d_0}\right|
 \le\frac{z|\det T|}{d_0}.$$ Taking the largest possible real part and smallest possible imaginary part in this disk, and using <a href="#free:exterior-forms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:exterior-forms">[free:exterior-forms]</a>, proves the assertions. All $Z$ in <a href="#free:disk" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:disk">[free:disk]</a> exceed $2.704$. Finally $Q'/Q=(ir/2)j$ gives <a href="#free:radial-quotients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="free:radial-quotients">[free:radial-quotients]</a>. ◻

</details>

Proposition <a href="#free:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:main">3</a> now follows from Lemmas <a href="#free:high-angular" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:high-angular">8</a>, <a href="#free:winding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:winding">10</a>, <a href="#free:profile-degree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:profile-degree">11</a>, and <a href="#free:exterior" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:exterior">12</a>.

<a id="sec:profiles"></a>
### Smooth profiles with a pressurized core

We work in dimension $12$. The small parameter is $a>0$; later we fix $a=(p-1)^{-1}$ with $p$ an odd integer. In this section $a$ may take every sufficiently small positive real value. The profiles never vanish, so the nonlinearity is smooth along them even without the restriction on $p$. We use the parameter disk from Proposition <a href="#free:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:main">3</a>; all terminating decimals below denote exact rational numbers. Thus $$\mathcal D=\{(b,Z):|(b,Z)-(b_*,Z_*)|\le10^{-8}\},\qquad
 b_*=.33477606871236,\quad Z_*=2.70506819293654.$$ Put <a id="profile:radii"></a>$$\label{profile:radii}
 \varepsilon=10^{-4},\qquad r_b=2\sqrt{Z_*}+\varepsilon,
 \qquad R(Z)=2\sqrt Z,\qquad r_0=r_b-6\varepsilon.$$ The radius $r_b$ is fixed throughout the construction.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Similarity variables and the amplitude equations

<a id="profile:similarity"></a>
<a id="profile:amplitude"></a>
<a id="profile:divergence"></a>
For $\tau=T-t$, $s=\log(T/\tau)$ and $y=(x-x_*)/\sqrt\tau$, write $$u(t,x)=e^{i\theta}\tau^{-a}(\tau/T)^{ib}U(s,y).$$ Since $\partial_t s=\tau^{-1}$ and $\partial_t y=y/(2\tau)$, substitution into the defocusing equation gives $$\label{profile:similarity}
 i\partial_sU+\Delta U+i\left(\frac{y\cdot\nabla}{2}+a\right)U+bU
       =|U|^{1/a}U.$$ We seek a nonvanishing radial stationary solution $Q=Ae^{i\phi}$, with $A>0$ and $\phi(0)=0$. Throughout this section a prime denotes a radial derivative, except when explicitly attached to the last argument of $H$. Set <a id="profile:notation"></a>$$\label{profile:notation}
 P=A^{1/a},\quad \mu=A^2,\quad c_*=6-2a,\quad
 w=\frac r2+2\phi',\quad D=r\partial_r.$$ The same letter $w$ denotes the vector field $w(r)y/r$, extended smoothly at zero, and $W=w\cdot\nabla$. The imaginary part of the stationary equation is $$2\frac{A'}A\phi'+\phi''+\frac{11}{r}\phi'
       +\frac r2\frac{A'}A+a=0.$$ It follows that $$\label{profile:amplitude}
 \begin{split}
 w(r)&=c_*r^{-11}A(r)^{-2}\int_0^r A(t)^2t^{11}\,dt,\\
 -\Delta A+A^{1+1/a}&=VA,\qquad
 V=b+\frac{r^2}{16}-\frac{w^2}{4},\\
 w'&=c_*-\frac{11w}{r}-2w\frac{A'}A.
 \end{split}$$ Here the integration constant in the first line is zero by regularity at the origin. The real part gives the second line because $\phi'^2+r\phi'/2=w^2/4-r^2/16$. In particular, $$\label{profile:divergence}
 \operatorname{div}(\mu w)=c_*\mu.$$ Conversely, positive amplitudes satisfying the first two equations in <a href="#profile:amplitude" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:amplitude">[profile:amplitude]</a>, with $A'(0)=0$, give a stationary solution by integrating $\phi'=(w-r/2)/2$.

Here is the complete profile statement used subsequently. A constant with a subscript $j$ may depend on that fixed derivative order. Uniform statements as $a\downarrow0$ refer to all profiles selected by the construction, without requiring a continuous selection of their matching parameters.

<a id="stmt-profile-existence"></a>
<div id="profile:existence" class="proposition">

**Proposition 13** (Profiles and uniform estimates). *There is $a_0>0$ such that, for every $0<a<a_0$, there are $(b_a,Z_a)\in\mathcal D$ and a smooth nonvanishing radial stationary solution $Q_a=A_ae^{i\phi_a}$ of <a href="#profile:similarity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:similarity">[profile:similarity]</a>, normalized by $Q_a(0)>0$. They have the following properties.*

<a id="profile:coercive-bounds"></a>
1.  *The identities <a href="#profile:amplitude" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:amplitude">[profile:amplitude]</a>–<a href="#profile:divergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:divergence">[profile:divergence]</a> hold, $0<P_a\le1$, and there are constants $c,C>0$, independent of small $a$, such that $$\label{profile:coercive-bounds}
     C^{-1}\langle r\rangle^{-4a}\le\mu_a(r)
           \le C\langle r\rangle^{-4a},\qquad
     \nabla w_a\ge c I.$$*

<a id="profile:limit"></a>
2.  *If $a_n\downarrow0$ and $(b_{a_n},Z_{a_n})\to(b,Z)$, then $j(b,Z)=0$, where $j$ is the free matching function of Proposition <a href="#free:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:main">3</a>. With $R=2\sqrt Z$, the profiles converge in $C^1$ on every compact ball to $$\label{profile:limit}
     Q_0(r)=
     \begin{cases}
     1,&0\le r\le R,\\[2pt]
     \displaystyle\frac{H(-ib,6,-ir^2/4)}{H(-ib,6,-iZ)},&r\ge R.
     \end{cases}$$ The exterior amplitude is strictly decreasing and has a positive limit at infinity. The convergence is $C^\infty$ on compact subsets of $\{r>R\}$. On every fixed ball, <a id="profile:pressure-limit"></a>$$\label{profile:pressure-limit}
     P_{a_n}\ \stackrel{*}{\rightharpoonup}\ b\mathbf1_{\{r<R\}}
           \quad\hbox{in }L^\infty,$$ and $\mu_{a_n},w_{a_n}$ converge in $C^1$. In particular $P_{a_n}/a_n\to0$ locally uniformly on $\{r>R\}$.*

<a id="profile:tail"></a>
<a id="profile:symbol-expansion"></a>
<a id="profile:exterior-pressure"></a>
<a id="profile:pressure-transport"></a>
3.  *On the fixed exterior $r\ge r_b$, $$\label{profile:tail}
     Q_a(r)=r^{\nu_a}f_a(r),\qquad \nu_a=-2a+2ib_a,
     \qquad 0<\kappa\le|f_a(r)|\le\rho<1,$$ with $\kappa,\rho$ independent of $a$. There are coefficients $m_{a,j}$, with $|m_{a,0}|\ge\kappa$, such that for every pair of fixed nonnegative integers $N,J$, $$\label{profile:symbol-expansion}
     \left|D^j\left(f_a(r)-\sum_{q=0}^{N}m_{a,q}r^{-2q}\right)\right|
           \le C_{N,J}r^{-2N-2},\qquad 0\le j\le J,\quad r\ge r_b.$$ The constants are uniform as $a\downarrow0$. In particular, $$\label{profile:exterior-pressure}
     |D^j(P_a/a)|\le\eta_j(a)r^{-2},\qquad
     |D^je^{2i\phi_a}|\le C_j,\qquad \eta_j(a)\longrightarrow0$$ for each fixed $j$. On the inner ball, $$\label{profile:pressure-transport}
     W_a(P_a/a)\le Ca(P_a/a),\qquad r\le r_b,$$ whereas $|W_a(P_a/a)|\le\eta(a)r^{-2}$ for $r\ge r_b$, with $\eta(a)\to0$. Consequently the positive part of $W_a(P_a/a)$ is bounded uniformly on the whole space.*

<a id="profile:fixed-power-symbols"></a>
<a id="profile:time-symbol"></a>
4.  *For each fixed $a$, every Cartesian derivative obeys $$\label{profile:fixed-power-symbols}
     |\partial^\beta Q_a(y)|\le C_{a,\beta}\langle y\rangle^{-2a-|\beta|},
     \qquad
     \left|\partial^\beta\big((P_a/a)e^{2i\phi_a}\big)\right|
           +|\partial^\beta(P_a/a)|
           \le C_{a,\beta}\langle y\rangle^{-2-|\beta|}.$$ The time-symmetry combination has the improved exterior order $$\label{profile:time-symbol}
     \left|D^j\left((a-ib_a+D/2)Q_a\right)\right|
           \le C_j r^{-2a-2},\qquad r\ge r_b.$$*

</div>

We prove the proposition in stages. The inner problem is solved on the whole parameter disk, as is the slow exterior problem. Only their final matching uses a choice of $(b_a,Z_a)$.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### The inner boundary value problem

<a id="profile:free-initial"></a>
Let $F(r;b,R')$ solve $$\label{profile:free-initial}
 F''+\left(\frac{11}{r}+\frac{ir}{2}\right)F'+bF=0,
       \qquad F(R')=1,\quad F'(R')=0,$$ and set $$F_{\rm in}(r;b,Z)=F(r;b,R(Z)),\qquad
 h_b(b,Z)=|F_{\rm in}(r_b;b,Z)|.$$ The subscript on $h_b$ indicates the fixed boundary radius, not dependence on $b$ alone.

<a id="stmt-profile-boundary-modulus"></a>
<div id="profile:boundary-modulus" class="lemma">

<a id="profile:boundary-gap"></a>
**Lemma 14** (The boundary modulus). *Uniformly on $\mathcal D$, $$\label{profile:boundary-gap}
 1-.2\varepsilon^2<h_b<1-.15\varepsilon^2.$$ For fixed $b$ in the projection of $\mathcal D$, the function $R'\mapsto|F(r_b;b,R')|$ is strictly increasing on $[r_0,r_b)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-profile-boundary-modulus"><summary>Proof of stmt-profile-boundary-modulus</summary>

*Proof.* On these intervals the coefficient $11/r+ir/2$ has modulus less than $6$, and $.334<b<.335$. Put $u=r-R'$, so $0\le u\le6\varepsilon$. Integrating the two first-order equations in <a href="#profile:free-initial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:free-initial">[profile:free-initial]</a> gives, by a bootstrap starting at $u=0$, $$|F'|\le .35u,\qquad |F-1|\le .175u^2,
       \qquad |F''+b|<.003.$$ For completeness, assuming the first bound up to a first failure gives $|F-1|\le.175u^2$ and $|F''+b|\le6(.35)u+.335(.175)u^2<.0013$; integration then gives $|F'|<.337u<.35u$, excluding that failure. The displayed weaker $.003$ bound follows as well. Thus $$F(r_b)=1-\frac b2(r_b-R')^2+E,\qquad
       |E|\le.0015(r_b-R')^2.$$ Since $|R(Z)-2\sqrt{Z_*}|<10^{-8}$, taking $R'=R(Z)$ proves <a href="#profile:boundary-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:boundary-gap">[profile:boundary-gap]</a>, including the negligible quadratic error when passing from the complex value to its modulus.

Differentiate the initial-value problem with respect to $R'$. The derivative $G=\partial_{R'}F$ satisfies the same linear equation and has data $G(R')=0$, $G'(R')=b$. Integration, or the same bootstrap, gives $|G(r_b)/(r_b-R')-b|<.003$. As $F(r_b)=1+O((r_b-R')^2)$, $$\partial_{R'}|F(r_b)|
   =\frac{\operatorname{Re}(\overline{F(r_b)}G(r_b))}{|F(r_b)|}>0.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-profile-inner"></a>
<div id="profile:inner" class="lemma">

<a id="profile:inner-bounds"></a>
<a id="profile:bulk-barrier"></a>
**Lemma 15** (The inner amplitude). *For all sufficiently small $a>0$ and all $(b,Z)\in\mathcal D$, the system <a href="#profile:amplitude" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:amplitude">[profile:amplitude]</a> has a positive radial solution on $[0,r_b]$ with $A'(0)=0$ and $A(r_b)=h_b$. Within the bounds $$\label{profile:inner-bounds}
 h_b\le A\le .5^a,$$ it is unique. It depends continuously on $(b,Z)$, and obeys $$\label{profile:bulk-barrier}
 A(r)\ge .2^a-.02(r-r_0)_+^2.$$ The amplitudes are bounded uniformly in $C^{1,1}[0,r_b]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-profile-inner"><summary>Proof of stmt-profile-inner</summary>

<a id="profile:w-ratio"></a>
<a id="profile:V-bounds"></a>
*Proof.* First take $a$ small enough that $h_b^{1/a}\le.3$ uniformly on the disk; this also ensures $h_b<.5^a$. For a continuous radial input $A$ satisfying <a href="#profile:inner-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:inner-bounds">[profile:inner-bounds]</a>, define $w,V$ by the first and second lines of <a href="#profile:amplitude" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:amplitude">[profile:amplitude]</a>. The useful nonsingular formula is $$\label{profile:w-ratio}
 \frac{w(r)}r=c_* A(r)^{-2}\int_0^1A(rs)^2s^{11}\,ds.$$ Since $h_b>.999$, $r_b^2<11$, and $a$ is small, it implies $$\label{profile:V-bounds}
 .3\le V\le .5,\qquad
 \|V[A_1]-V[A_2]\|_\infty\le10\|A_1-A_2\|_\infty.$$ Indeed $c_*h_b^2/12\le w/r\le c_*/(12h_b^2)$; inserting these bounds into $V=b+r^2(1/16-(w/r)^2/4)$ gives the first assertion for small $a$. The Lipschitz constant of $A\mapsto w/r$ is at most $c_*(2h_b^{-3}+2h_b^{-2})/12<2.01$; multiplying by $r_b^2(\sup w/r)/2$ gives a constant less than $10$ for $V$.

<a id="profile:scalar-dirichlet"></a>
For this prescribed $V$, solve the scalar Dirichlet problem $$\label{profile:scalar-dirichlet}
 -\Delta H+H^{1+1/a}=VH,\qquad H(r_b)=h_b,\quad H'(0)=0.$$ The constants $h_b$ and $.5^a$ are respectively a lower and an upper solution. One can obtain $H$ by monotone iteration: choose $L>0$ so that $t\mapsto(L+V(r))t-t^{1+1/a}$ is increasing on $[h_b,.5^a]$, and at each step invert $-\Delta+L$ with the given boundary value. This inverse is positive by the minimum principle. Its existence, if needed directly in the radial class, follows by minimizing the strictly convex functional $\int(|\nabla u|^2+L|u|^2-2fu)/2$ for zero boundary data; the radial equation then reads $(r^{11}u')'=r^{11}(Lu-f)$ and gives the classical solution. Iteration from $h_b$ is increasing and stays below $.5^a$. For fixed $a$ its right sides are uniformly bounded; radial integration gives compactness in $C^1$, and its limit solves <a href="#profile:scalar-dirichlet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:scalar-dirichlet">[profile:scalar-dirichlet]</a>.

We record a comparison argument that also applies below. The radial Dirichlet inverse of $-\Delta$ is $$(Kf)(r)=\int_r^{r_b}t^{-11}\int_0^t s^{11}f(s)\,ds\,dt,
       \qquad \|K\|_{\infty\to\infty}=r_b^2/24<11/24.$$ Thus the positive geometric series defines $T=(-\Delta-.5)^{-1}=\sum_{n\ge0}(.5K)^nK$ with zero boundary values. Also $\psi=1+K(1+.5\psi)$ has a positive solution and satisfies $(-\Delta-.5)\psi=1$, $\psi(r_b)=1$. For every $q\ge0$ and $V\le.5$, $$(-\Delta-V+q)\psi\ge1.$$ If a function with nonnegative boundary values violated the comparison principle for $-\Delta-V+q$, its ratio to $\psi$ would have a negative interior minimum, where the product rule gives a strictly negative value for that operator. This is a contradiction. Taking $q$ to be the divided difference of $t^{1+1/a}$ proves uniqueness in <a href="#profile:scalar-dirichlet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:scalar-dirichlet">[profile:scalar-dirichlet]</a> and comparison with lower solutions.

The function $B(r)=.2^a-.02(r-r_0)_+^2$ is such a lower solution. For small $a$ it is positive, $B(r_b)<h_b$ by <a href="#profile:boundary-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:boundary-gap">[profile:boundary-gap]</a>, $B^{1/a}\le.2$, and $$-\Delta B\le .04+.44\frac{6\varepsilon}{r_0}<.05
       \le(.3-.2)B.$$ At $r_0$ the first derivative is continuous, so no interface measure occurs in this inequality. Comparison proves <a href="#profile:bulk-barrier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:bulk-barrier">[profile:bulk-barrier]</a> for every output $H$.

Let $H_1,H_2$ be outputs for two inputs. Their difference solves $$(-\Delta-V_1+q)(H_1-H_2)=(V_1-V_2)H_2,
 \quad
 q=\int_0^1(1+1/a)(tH_1+(1-t)H_2)^{1/a}\,dt.$$ The barrier implies $q\ge.2/a$ on $r\le r_0$, whereas $q\ge0$ everywhere. A positive supersolution for unit forcing is <a id="profile:small-inverse"></a>$$\label{profile:small-inverse}
 S=10a+2T\mathbf1_{\{r>r_0\}}.$$ On the inner ball, the constant term contributes at least $2-5a$ and the second term contributes a nonnegative quantity. On the shell, the second term contributes at least $2$, while the constant term is bounded below by $-5a$. Thus the comparison principle applies, including at the shell interface. To bound $S$, radial integration gives $$\|K\mathbf1_{\{r>r_0\}}\|_\infty
 \le\int_{r_0}^{r_b}(t-r_0)\,dt=(6\varepsilon)^2/2,
 \qquad
 \|T\mathbf1_{\{r>r_0\}}\|_\infty\le(6\varepsilon)^2.$$ Here the second inequality uses $(1-.5\|K\|)^{-1}<2$. Consequently the input-output map satisfies $$\|H_1-H_2\|_\infty
 \le10\big(10a+2(6\varepsilon)^2\big)\|A_1-A_2\|_\infty.$$ Our already fixed shell width makes the second contribution less than $1/4$; taking $a$ small makes the whole factor less than $1/2$. The closed interval <a href="#profile:inner-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:inner-bounds">[profile:inner-bounds]</a> in the radial continuous functions is complete, so successive iteration gives its unique fixed point. This proves the coupled inner problem.

Finally $0<P\le.5$ and <a href="#profile:V-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:V-bounds">[profile:V-bounds]</a> give $$A'(r)=r^{-11}\int_0^r t^{11}(P(t)-V(t))A(t)\,dt,
       \qquad |A'|\le Cr,\quad |A''|\le C.$$ These are the asserted uniform bounds. For fixed $a$, convergent parameter sequences have $C^1$ convergent subsequences; the equation and uniqueness identify their limits. This proves continuous dependence, including when the boundary value $h_b$ varies. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-profile-core-limit"></a>
<div id="profile:core-limit" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (Identification of the core). *For the inner solutions of Lemma <a href="#profile:inner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="profile:inner">15</a>, suppose $a_n\downarrow0$ and $(b_n,Z_n)\to(b,Z)\in\mathcal D$. Then their complex reconstructions converge in $C^1[0,r_b]$ to the function equal to $1$ on $[0,R(Z)]$ and to $F_{\rm in}$ on $[R(Z),r_b]$. Their pressures converge weak star to $b\mathbf1_{\{r<R(Z)\}}$. These convergence assertions are uniform over the compact parameter disk in the sequential sense just stated.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-profile-core-limit"><summary>Proof of stmt-profile-core-limit</summary>

*Proof.* The preceding bounds give $C^1$ compactness of the amplitudes. Every limit $A_0$ is at most $1$ and, by <a href="#profile:bulk-barrier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:bulk-barrier">[profile:bulk-barrier]</a>, equals $1$ on $[0,r_0]$. Formula <a href="#profile:w-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:w-ratio">[profile:w-ratio]</a> and the last equation in <a href="#profile:amplitude" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:amplitude">[profile:amplitude]</a> give $C^1$ convergence of $w$, including at zero. On every compact interval where $A_0<1$, the pressure tends uniformly to zero. There the limit satisfies $\Delta A_0=-V_0A_0$, with $V_0\ge.3$.

Each component of $\{A_0<1\}$ has a left endpoint $R'$ where $A_0(R')=1$, $A_0'(R')=0$. On this component $(r^{11}A_0')'=-r^{11}V_0A_0<0$, so $A_0'<0$ and the component cannot have a right endpoint below $r_b$. As $A_0(r_b)=h_b<1$, there is exactly one such component, $(R',r_b]$, and $R'\in[r_0,r_b)$. On the plateau <a href="#profile:w-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:w-ratio">[profile:w-ratio]</a> gives $w_0=r/2$ and hence $\phi_0=0$. Reconstruction gives the free complex equation with data $1,0$ at $R'$. Its boundary modulus is $h_b$, so Lemma <a href="#profile:boundary-modulus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="profile:boundary-modulus">14</a> forces $R'=R(Z)$.

The pressures are bounded by $.5$. Any weak-star limit $P_0$ satisfies in distributions $$P_0A_0=V_0A_0+\Delta A_0.$$ There is no surface measure at $R$ because $A_0$ and $A_0'$ are continuous. On the plateau the right side is $b$, and on its exterior it is zero. Since $A_0$ is positive, this identifies $P_0$ uniquely. The preceding identification applies to every subsequence; compactness therefore proves all convergence assertions. Reconstruction by $\phi'=(w-r/2)/2$ gives the asserted $C^1$ convergence of the complex profiles as well. ◻

</details>

<figure id="fig:core-limit">

<figcaption>Schematic of the singular profile limit as $a → 0$. The amplitude $A_{0}$ equals $1$ for $r ≤ R$ and decreases strictly on the free exterior; its limiting tail is nonzero. The pressure converges weakly to $P_{0} = b1_{{r < R}}$; its value at the jump is immaterial. Compact support of this limiting pressure does not assert compact support of the profiles $Q$. The spectral interface conditions for amplitude and phase perturbations are derived in Lemma <a href="#spec:interface" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:interface">30</a>. Heights and radial distances are schematic.</figcaption>
</figure>
