Visual witness description of PDF page 18 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Two schematic 2D Cartesian coordinate plots side-by-side. Left plot labeled "(a) Limiting amplitude" shows vertical axis $A_0(r)$ with values 0 and 1, and horizontal axis $r$ with values 0 and $R$. The curve is constant at height 1 from $r = 0$ to $r = R$ (region below labeled "constant core"), followed by a smooth strictly decreasing curve for $r > R$ asymptotically approaching a positive horizontal level (region above labeled "free exterior strictly decreasing"). Right plot labeled "(b) Limiting pressure" shows vertical axis $P_0(r)$ with values 0 and $b$, and horizontal axis $r$ with values 0 and $R$. A flat horizontal line sits at height $b$ from $r = 0$ to $r = R$ (labeled "pressure $b$"), drops abruptly at $r = R$ via a vertical dashed line, and continues at height 0 along the horizontal axis for $r > R$ (labeled "zero pressure").
```


The pressure limit in Figure <a href="#fig:core-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:core-limit">1</a> is compatible with $A_a\to1$ in the core: the power $1/a$ retains the order-$a$ variation of the amplitude. Weak-star convergence, rather than differentiation of this limiting step function, is the fact needed in the constrained mode problem.

For each $(b,Z)\in\mathcal D$, the inner solution now has prescribed boundary modulus $h_b$ and a determined complex logarithmic derivative at $r_b$. We next construct a slow exterior solution with the same boundary modulus. A constant phase rotation can then match the boundary values; equality of the logarithmic derivatives supplies the two remaining real matching conditions. Lemma <a href="#profile:matching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="profile:matching">18</a> will impose these conditions by varying $(b,Z)$.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### A uniform slow solution on the exterior

Write $$G_b(r)=H(-ib,6,-ir^2/4),\qquad
 j(b,z)=-\frac{\partial_xH(-ib,6,-iz)}{H(-ib,6,-iz)}.$$ Proposition <a href="#free:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:main">3</a> says that $G_b$ never vanishes for $r\ge R(Z)$. For its amplitude and phase, <a id="profile:free-j"></a>$$\label{profile:free-j}
 \frac{A'}A=-\frac r2\operatorname{Im}j(b,r^2/4),\qquad
 \frac wr=\frac12+\operatorname{Re}j(b,r^2/4)<.543.$$ At $R$, $w/r>0$ because $|j(b,Z)|\le3\cdot10^{-9}$. The divergence equation with $c_*=6$ shows that $w$ stays positive. For $R\le r\le2\sqrt3$, it follows that $$V=b+r^2/16-w^2/4>.18.$$ Normalize temporarily by $|G_b(R)|=1$. Its initial amplitude derivative has magnitude at most $(R/2)3\cdot10^{-9}$, and the equation $(r^{11}A')'=-r^{11}VA$ makes $A'(r_b)<0$: on $[R,r_b]$ the amplitude stays, for example, between $1/2$ and $2$, while integrating the negative right side decreases $r^{11}A'$ by more than its initial magnitude. It continues to decrease up to $2\sqrt3$. Beyond that radius the strict inequality $\operatorname{Im}j>0$ proves $A'<0$ directly. Thus $|G_b|$ is strictly decreasing on $[r_b,\infty)$, uniformly over the disk in the sense of strict negativity on each compact interval. If $j(b,Z)=0$, the same argument gives strict decrease throughout $(R,\infty)$.

<a id="profile:normalized-free"></a>
For reference define the normalized free exterior $$\label{profile:normalized-free}
 Q^{\rm out}_0(r;b,Z)=h_b(b,Z)\frac{G_b(r)}{G_b(r_b)}.$$ It has positive real value $h_b$ at $r_b$. The free symbol expansion shows that $r^{-2ib}Q^{\rm out}_0$ tends to a nonzero number $m_0(b,Z)$. By the preceding monotonicity its modulus is at most $h_b<1$ everywhere. Nonvanishing, the nonzero limit, and compactness of the parameter disk therefore give uniform lower and upper bounds strictly between $0$ and $1$ for its modulus.

<a id="stmt-profile-exterior"></a>
<div id="profile:exterior" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (Slow exterior construction). *For all sufficiently small $a>0$ and $(b,Z)\in\mathcal D$, there is a nonvanishing exterior stationary solution $Q^{\rm out}_a$ with $Q^{\rm out}_a(r_b)=h_b$. It depends continuously on $(a,b,Z)$, down to the limit <a href="#profile:normalized-free" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:normalized-free">[profile:normalized-free]</a>, with convergence in every fixed derivative on compact exterior intervals. Its factor $f=r^{2a-2ib}Q^{\rm out}_a$ satisfies the bounds and expansions in <a href="#profile:tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:tail">[profile:tail]</a>–<a href="#profile:symbol-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:symbol-expansion">[profile:symbol-expansion]</a>, uniformly over the entire parameter disk.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-profile-exterior"><summary>Proof of stmt-profile-exterior</summary>

<a id="profile:f-equation"></a>
<a id="profile:annulus"></a>
*Proof.* Writing $\nu=-2a+2ib$ and $Q=r^\nu f$ transforms the equation into $$\label{profile:f-equation}
 (D+\nu)(D+\nu+10)f+\frac{ir^2}{2}Df=N_a(f),
       \qquad N_a(f)=|f|^{1/a}f.$$ Set $N_0=0$. Choose a small fixed neighborhood of the compact family $(m_0(b,Z),b)$ such that the corresponding free functions $f$ stay in an annulus $$\label{profile:annulus}
 0<2\kappa\le|f|\le\rho_0<1,\qquad r\ge r_b.$$ This is possible because at $a=0$ the equation is complex linear in the coefficient $m=\lim f$. Enlarge the annulus slightly, still keeping its upper radius below $1$. On this enlarged annulus every fixed real derivative of $N_a$ tends uniformly to zero as $a\downarrow0$: its norm is bounded by $C_j a^{-j}\rho_1^{1/a}$ for some $\rho_1<1$ (adjusting $C_j$ and the power of $a$ does not affect this conclusion). Thus all fixed differentiability orders needed below are uniformly controlled.

For prescribed $m,b$, construct a formal expansion $f_N=\sum_{q=0}^Nm_qr^{-2q}$ with $m_0=m$. Substituting in <a href="#profile:f-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:f-equation">[profile:f-equation]</a> determines $m_{q+1}$ from the coefficient of $r^{-2q}$: its coefficient in $(ir^2/2)Df_N$ is $-i(q+1)m_{q+1}$. All the other terms at that order involve only $m_0,\ldots,m_q$ and derivatives of $N_a$ at $m$. This proves existence and uniqueness of the coefficients, boundedness at every fixed order, continuous dependence down to $a=0$, and the same bounds after a fixed number of derivatives in the real and imaginary parts of $m$ and in $b$. The residual of $f_N$ is $O(r^{-2N})$, with its fixed symbol and parameter derivatives.

<a id="profile:tail-system"></a>
<a id="profile:tail-contraction"></a>
Here is a convergent construction behind this expansion. Put $t=\log r$ and $Y=(e,De)$ for $e=f-f_N$. The error equation has the form $$\label{profile:tail-system}
 \partial_tY=
 \begin{pmatrix}0&0\\0&-ie^{2t}/2\end{pmatrix}Y
       +\mathcal B Y+\mathcal N(t,Y)+\mathcal R_N(t),$$ where the constant matrix $\mathcal B$ has bounded norm, $\mathcal N(t,0)=0$, its Lipschitz constant on the annulus is bounded uniformly in $a,m,b$, and $|\mathcal R_N(t)|\le C_Ne^{-2Nt}$. The propagator $\Phi(t,s)$ of the displayed diagonal part is unitary. The backward integral equation is $$\label{profile:tail-contraction}
 Y(t)=-\int_t^\infty\Phi(t,s)
       \big(\mathcal B Y(s)+\mathcal N(s,Y(s))+\mathcal R_N(s)\big)\,ds.$$ On $t\ge t_*$ use the norm $\|Y\|_N=\sup e^{2Nt}|Y(t)|$. If $C$ bounds the combined Lipschitz constant of the two terms involving $Y$, their integral has norm at most $C/(2N)$. Choose one integer $N_*$ with this ratio below $1/2$, and then choose $t_*$ large enough that the corresponding ball around $f_{N_*}$ remains in the enlarged annulus. The residual term has norm at most $C_{N_*}/(2N_*)$, so iteration in a ball of twice this radius converges geometrically to a solution. No decay estimate for an oscillatory propagator has been used; unitarity and the weight make the integral absolutely convergent.

The same construction works at any larger truncation order. Two solutions so obtained differ by $O(r^{-2N_*})$ in both entries after subtracting their common expansion through order $N_*$. Their difference solves <a href="#profile:tail-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:tail-contraction">[profile:tail-contraction]</a> without the residual, on a common sufficiently long tail. The contraction estimate forces it to vanish there, and uniqueness for the finite-interval ODE makes the solutions identical. This proves an expansion to every order for the single solution already constructed. Symbol derivatives cause no loss in this conclusion. Indeed <a href="#profile:tail-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:tail-system">[profile:tail-system]</a> bounds the next derivative of an error by at most $C r^2$ times its two entries and the residual; differentiating it $J$ times loses at most $2J$ powers of $r$. Constructing at an order greater than $N+J+2$ first therefore gives the remainder estimate of <a href="#profile:symbol-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:symbol-expansion">[profile:symbol-expansion]</a> through the prescribed order $J$. This also explains why the constants need only be uniform at each fixed order.

Differentiating the integral equation with respect to $m$ or $b$ gives the same contraction for its derivative, with a bounded inhomogeneous term. Difference quotients converge to that solution, proving real $C^1$ dependence; repeated differentiation works at every fixed order. Continuity at $a=0$ follows directly from the uniform contraction and convergence of its coefficients. Continue the solution from the fixed large radius back to $r_b$. On this finite interval the right side of the first-order form of <a href="#profile:f-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:f-equation">[profile:f-equation]</a> and all its fixed derivatives are bounded and converge on the enlarged annulus. The integral equation on finitely many short intervals, followed by Gronwall’s inequality for differences, keeps the solution close to its free counterpart and proves existence and parameter convergence down to $r_b$. This also proves convergence of all fixed radial derivatives and the first parameter derivatives there. In view of <a href="#profile:annulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:annulus">[profile:annulus]</a>, the final functions satisfy $\kappa\le|f|\le\rho<1$ for fixed $\kappa,\rho$, uniformly in small $a$ and the chosen parameter neighborhood.

It remains to choose $m$ so that $Q(r_b)=h_b$. At $a=0$ the map $m\mapsto Q(r_b)$ is complex multiplication by a nonzero factor. Its real two-dimensional inverse is uniformly bounded over the compact parameter disk. The just proved $C^1$ convergence implies that, on a fixed small neighborhood of each $m_0$, the equation for $m$ is a contraction after multiplying by that limiting inverse. Its residual at $m_0$ tends to zero uniformly, so it has a unique root near $m_0$. These roots agree on overlapping neighborhoods and depend continuously on $(a,b,Z)$. This imposes the required boundary value. All tail bounds extend from the initially fixed large radius to $r_b$ by the compact interval bounds, completing the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Matching, pressure transport, and deformation

<a id="stmt-profile-matching"></a>
<div id="profile:matching" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (Matching by degree). *For every sufficiently small $a>0$ there is $(b_a,Z_a)\in\mathcal D$ at which the inner and outer profiles have the same logarithmic derivative at $r_b$. After a constant phase rotation of the outer profile they form a smooth nonvanishing stationary profile. Every parameter limit as $a\downarrow0$ is a zero of $j$ in $\mathcal D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-profile-matching"><summary>Proof of stmt-profile-matching</summary>

*Proof.* Let $Q^{\rm in}_a$ be reconstructed with phase zero at the origin, and define the continuous complex-valued matching map $$M_a(b,Z)=\frac{(Q^{\rm in}_a)'(r_b)}{Q^{\rm in}_a(r_b)}
       -\frac{(Q^{\rm out}_a)'(r_b)}{Q^{\rm out}_a(r_b)}.$$ Lemmas <a href="#profile:core-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="profile:core-limit">16</a> and <a href="#profile:exterior" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="profile:exterior">17</a> give uniform convergence on $\mathcal D$ to $M_0=F_{\rm in}'(r_b)/F_{\rm in}(r_b)-G_b'(r_b)/G_b(r_b)$. Both denominators are nonzero.

<a id="profile:wronskian-match"></a>
The Wronskian $\mathcal W=F_{\rm in}G_b'-F_{\rm in}'G_b$ satisfies $\mathcal W'=-(11/r+ir/2)\mathcal W$. At $R=2\sqrt Z$, $G_b'(R)=(iR/2)j(b,Z)G_b(R)$, and hence $$\label{profile:wronskian-match}
 M_0(b,Z)=K(b,Z)j(b,Z),\qquad
 K=-\frac{iR}{2}\left(\frac R{r_b}\right)^{11}
 e^{-i(r_b^2-R^2)/4}
 \frac{G_b(R)}{F_{\rm in}(r_b)G_b(r_b)}.$$ The factor $K$ is continuous and never zero on the entire disk. Contracting its parameter argument to the disk center gives a homotopy through nonzero factors to a constant. Complex multiplication by that constant preserves orientation. Proposition <a href="#free:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:main">3</a> therefore gives $$\deg(M_0,\mathcal D^\circ,0)
       =\deg(j,\mathcal D^\circ,0)\ne0.$$ In particular $M_0$ is separated from zero on the boundary. Uniform convergence preserves the boundary homotopy and the degree for every sufficiently small $a$, so $M_a$ has a zero.

At such a zero the values have equal modulus $h_b$. Rotate the outer profile to match the value of the inner one; equality of logarithmic derivatives then matches the first derivatives as well. The ODE gives smoothness across $r_b$, since the profile is nonzero. To verify smoothness through the origin without a regular-singular shortcut, write $q(x)=Q(\sqrt x)$ for $x\ge0$. Integrating the complex radial equation gives $$q'(x)=\frac14\int_0^1t^5e^{-ix(1-t)/4}
       \big(|q(xt)|^{1/a}-b-ia\big)q(xt)\,dt.$$ The right side is continuous at $x=0$. It first proves $q\in C^1$ and then, by successive differentiation, $q\in C^\infty$ because $q$ is bounded away from zero on this compact interval. Thus $Q(y)=q(|y|^2)$ is smooth at the origin. The normalization at zero was already imposed. Finally, a convergent sequence of matching parameters satisfies $M_0(b,Z)=0$, so <a href="#profile:wronskian-match" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:wronskian-match">[profile:wronskian-match]</a> gives $j(b,Z)=0$. At such a root the free initial-value solution equals $G_b/G_b(R)$, proving <a href="#profile:limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:limit">[profile:limit]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-profile-pressure"></a>
<div id="profile:pressure" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (The pressure derivative and positive deformation). *The matched profiles satisfy <a href="#profile:coercive-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:coercive-bounds">[profile:coercive-bounds]</a> and <a href="#profile:exterior-pressure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:exterior-pressure">[profile:exterior-pressure]</a>–<a href="#profile:pressure-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:pressure-transport">[profile:pressure-transport]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-profile-pressure"><summary>Proof of stmt-profile-pressure</summary>

*Proof.* First consider the inner amplitude. On every component of $\{P<.2\}$, $$(r^{11}A')'=r^{11}(P-V)A<0.$$ At a positive left endpoint $A'\le0$, since the amplitude is entering the sublevel set $A<.2^a$; at the origin $A'=0$. Thus $A'<0$ at every positive radius in this sublevel set. If $M=\max_{[0,r_b]}(A')_+$ is positive, it is attained at a point $r_m\in(0,r_b)$: the derivative is zero at the origin and strictly negative at $r_b$ for small $a$ by Lemma <a href="#profile:core-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="profile:core-limit">16</a>. At this point $P\ge.2$, $A''=0$, and $A'''\le0$.

Let $q=w(r_m)/r_m$. The bounds $A\le.5^a$, $A(r_m)\ge.2^a$ and <a href="#profile:w-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:w-ratio">[profile:w-ratio]</a> give $$.49<q\le q_+:=\frac{c_*}{12}(2.5)^{2a}=\frac12+O(a).$$ The fixed lower bound follows from $h_b>.999$ and small $a$; it need not approach $1/2$ at any prescribed rate. Differentiating $V$ and using <a href="#profile:amplitude" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:amplitude">[profile:amplitude]</a> gives the exact identity $$V'=r\left(\frac18-\frac{c_*}{2}\frac wr
                  +\frac{11}{2}\left(\frac wr\right)^2\right)
          +w^2\frac{A'}A.$$ The polynomial $q\mapsto1/8-c_*q/2+11q^2/2$ is increasing for $q\ge.49$ and small $a$, and its value at $q_+$ is $O(a)$, since its value at $(a,q)=(0,1/2)$ is zero. Thus at $r_m$, $$V'\le Ca+w^2M/A.$$ Differentiate the amplitude equation. At this maximum, $$(-\Delta A)'=-A'''-\frac{11}{r}A''+\frac{11}{r^2}A'\ge0,$$ so $$\frac{.2}{a}M\le VM+V'A\le CM+Ca.$$ It follows that $M\le Ca^2$. Since $w\ge0$ and $w/A$ is bounded on the inner ball, $$W(P/a)=\frac{wPA'}{a^2A}\le CP=Ca(P/a),$$ which proves <a href="#profile:pressure-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:pressure-transport">[profile:pressure-transport]</a>.

In the exterior, <a href="#profile:tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:tail">[profile:tail]</a> gives $P/a=r^{-2}|f|^{1/a}/a$. Repeated Euler differentiation and $\kappa\le|f|\le\rho<1$ bound every fixed derivative by $C_j a^{-N_j}\rho^{1/a}r^{-2}$ for some finite integer $N_j$. This tends to zero uniformly after multiplication by $r^2$. Also $e^{2i\phi}=r^{4ib}f/\overline f$, so all its fixed Euler derivatives are uniformly bounded. As $w/r$ is uniformly bounded in the exterior, these facts give <a href="#profile:exterior-pressure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:exterior-pressure">[profile:exterior-pressure]</a> and the exterior bound on $W(P/a)$. Together with $P\le.5$ in the interior they give the uniform upper bound on its positive part. By taking $a$ smaller if necessary, the exterior formula also gives $P\le1$ everywhere. The same bounds for $f$, and <a href="#profile:inner-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:inner-bounds">[profile:inner-bounds]</a>, prove the two-sided comparison for $\mu$ in <a href="#profile:coercive-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:coercive-bounds">[profile:coercive-bounds]</a>.

It remains to prove positive deformation. The two eigenvalues of the derivative of a radial field are $w'$ and $w/r$. For every limiting matched profile they equal $1/2$ in the core. On the exterior, $A_0'<0$, $w_0>0$, and $w_0/r<.543$, so $$w_0'=6-11w_0/r-2w_0A_0'/A_0\ge6-11(.543)=.027.$$ Integrating from zero also gives $w_0/r\ge.027$. The compact $C^1$ convergence of $A$ and formula <a href="#profile:w-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:w-ratio">[profile:w-ratio]</a>, followed by the last equation of <a href="#profile:amplitude" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:amplitude">[profile:amplitude]</a>, give convergence of both eigenvalues on every fixed compact interval, including zero. For the remaining tail, the uniform expansion of Lemma <a href="#profile:exterior" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="profile:exterior">17</a> gives $$\frac wr=\frac12+\frac{4b}{r^2}+O(r^{-4}),\qquad
 w'=\frac12-\frac{4b}{r^2}+O(r^{-4}),$$ uniformly in small $a$. Choose a large fixed radius to make these tail bounds positive, and only then take $a$ small enough for the compact convergence. A subsequence contradiction, using compactness of the parameter disk, makes this choice uniform for all matched profiles. For example $c=.01$ is admissible after reducing $a_0$. This proves <a href="#profile:coercive-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:coercive-bounds">[profile:coercive-bounds]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-profile-pressure-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-profile-pressure</summary>

*Completion of Proposition <a href="#profile:existence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="profile:existence">13</a>.* The inner construction, exterior construction and degree argument give existence for every $0<a<a_0$ after finitely many reductions of $a_0$. The order of choices is fixed: the disk and $\varepsilon$ first, then the core comparison estimates, the exterior annulus and a single large truncation order and starting radius, and finally sufficiently small $a$. Any later fixed derivative order is obtained by taking a higher auxiliary truncation of the same exterior solution; it does not require changing that solution or the already fixed geometric parameters.

The limiting statements on the inner ball are Lemma <a href="#profile:core-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="profile:core-limit">16</a>, and on $[r_b,\infty)$ follow from Lemma <a href="#profile:exterior" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="profile:exterior">17</a> and matching. On a compact subset of $(R,r_b)$, the limiting amplitude is strictly below $1$; uniform $C^1$ convergence therefore places the profiles in a fixed annulus bounded away from both $0$ and $1$. All derivatives of $z\mapsto|z|^{1/a}z$ tend to zero there. The complex radial ODE, successively differentiated, then proves $C^\infty$ convergence and $P/a\to0$ on that compact set. The weak-star pressure assertion on any larger fixed ball follows by joining the inner assertion to this exterior convergence. The $C^1$ convergence of $\mu,w$ has already been proved from their amplitude formulas.

Finally, radial Euler estimates imply Cartesian symbol estimates away from zero: an ordinary derivative of order $j$ is a linear combination of $r^{-j}$ times Euler derivatives with bounded smooth angular coefficients. The fixed-$a$ smoothness on a compact ball supplies the remaining bounds in <a href="#profile:fixed-power-symbols" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:fixed-power-symbols">[profile:fixed-power-symbols]</a>; their constants are not asserted to be uniform as $a\downarrow0$. As $a-ib+\nu/2=0$, $$(a-ib+D/2)Q=\tfrac12r^\nu Df,$$ and <a href="#profile:symbol-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:symbol-expansion">[profile:symbol-expansion]</a> gives <a href="#profile:time-symbol" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="profile:time-symbol">[profile:time-symbol]</a>. All parts of the proposition follow. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:spectrum"></a>
### The outgoing spectrum

Throughout this section, $Q=Ae^{i\phi}$ is one of the profiles of Proposition <a href="#profile:existence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="profile:existence">13</a>, and $$P=A^{1/a},\quad \mu=A^2,\quad c_*=6-2a,\quad
 w=(r/2+2\phi')\partial_r,\quad W=w\cdot\nabla,\quad D=r\partial_r.$$ The same letter $w$ denotes the radial component of the vector field when it appears in a one-dimensional formula. Complexification is always complexification of a real linear equation. Thus its two circular components $v_+,v_-$ are independent; the original real space is the subspace $v_- =\overline{v_+}$.

<a id="stmt-spectrum-classification"></a>
<div id="spectrum:classification" class="theorem">

**Theorem 20** (Classification of regular modes). *For all sufficiently small admissible $a>0$, the only regular outgoing modes with $\operatorname{Re}\lambda\ge-1/32$ are the phase, translation, and time modes. Their first circular components and eigenvalues are $$iQ\quad(\lambda=0),\qquad
 \partial_jQ\quad(\lambda=1/2, 1\le j\le12),\qquad
 (a-ib+D/2)Q\quad(\lambda=1).$$ The second component of each listed real symmetry generator is the conjugate of the displayed component. Their complex spans are the corresponding complexified eigenspaces. In each fixed spherical harmonic, each eigenvalue that occurs is algebraically simple; degrees $\ell\ge2$ have no such eigenvalue.*

<a id="spec:slow-condition"></a>
*Here “outgoing” means that, in each component, for every fixed integer $j\ge0$, $$\label{spec:slow-condition}
 D^jv_h=O(r^{-2(a+\operatorname{Re}\lambda)})\qquad(r\longrightarrow\infty).$$ The same classification holds for regular solutions for which $\partial_r^kv_h\in L^2(r^{11}\,dr)$ for some integer $k\ge7$. There is no regular generalized mode above any of these eigenvectors with this top-derivative integrability. Algebraic simplicity for outgoing modes means simplicity of the analytic regular/outgoing boundary problem, including its differentiated outgoing condition.*

</div>

We prove the theorem in several steps. All statements about $a\to0$ allow a convergent subsequence of the profile parameters $(b,Z)$. Uniform conclusions for all small $a$ will follow by contradiction and subsequence extraction, so a continuous choice of matched profiles is not needed.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Linearization and the boundary condition at infinity

<a id="spec:circular"></a>
<a id="spec:real-system"></a>
<a id="spec:divergence"></a>
Let a real spherical harmonic of degree $\ell$ have unit $L^2$ norm on $\mathbb S^{11}$. Its eigenvalue for $-\Delta_{\mathbb S^{11}}$ is $\ell(\ell+10)$. The mode equation in this harmonic is $$\label{spec:circular}
 v_h''+\left(\frac{11}{r}+\frac{hi r}{2}\right)v_h'
 +\left[b+hi(a+\lambda)-\frac{\ell(\ell+10)}{r^2}\right]v_h
 =Pv_h+\frac{P}{2a}(v_h+e^{2hi\phi}v_{-h}),\qquad h=\pm1.$$ Set $$v_h=Ae^{hi\phi}(f+hi g),\qquad X=(f,g)^t,
 \qquad J=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}.$$ We include the angular harmonic in $f,g$ when using spatial notation. The coordinates $f,g$ are independent complex variables in the spectral problem and are real on the original real space. Cancellation of the stationary profile terms gives $$\label{spec:real-system}
 \mathcal H X+J(\lambda+W)X=0,\qquad
 L=-\mu^{-1}\nabla\cdot(\mu\nabla),\qquad
 \mathcal H=\begin{pmatrix}L+P/a&0\\0&L\end{pmatrix}.$$ Indeed the terms remaining after division by $Ae^{hi\phi}$ in <a href="#spec:circular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:circular">[spec:circular]</a> are $-L+hiW$ and the additional pressure $(P/a)f$. The evolutionary generator in these variables is therefore $\mathcal G=J\mathcal H-W$. We use repeatedly $$\label{spec:divergence}
 \operatorname{div}(\mu w)=c_*\mu,
 \qquad W^*=-W-c_*$$ in the weighted volume inner product, with the appropriate boundary term on a ball.

<a id="stmt-spec-block-reduction"></a>
<div id="spec:block-reduction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21** (Block reduction at infinity). *Suppose that $$DY=(r^2\Lambda(r)+B(r))Y$$ on a half-line, where $\Lambda$ is diagonal, its selected blocks have uniformly separated spectra, and $\Lambda,B$ and their fixed Euler derivatives are bounded. For every fixed $m$ there is an invertible change $I+O_m(r^{-2})$, with the same symbol bounds, after which the off-block remainder is $O_m(r^{-m})$. The bounded part in each diagonal block changes by $O_m(r^{-2})$. The changes preserve holomorphic dependence on any parameters on which the original system depends holomorphically.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-block-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-block-reduction</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose an off-block entry is $b_{jk}=O(r^{-q})$. A change with entry $-r^{-2}b_{jk}/(\Lambda_j-\Lambda_k)$ cancels it by commutation with $r^2\Lambda$. Differentiating this change or multiplying it by the bounded part gives $O(r^{-q-2})$; its products with the other changes have the same or better order. The denominator and all its fixed Euler derivatives are uniformly bounded by the spectral gap. Perform this operation simultaneously on every off-block entry, and iterate a finite number of times. The product of the changes is invertible for sufficiently large $r$ by its $I+O_m(r^{-2})$ bound. Differentiating the construction proves the symbol estimates. All operations are algebraic operations and differentiation with nonzero denominators, proving the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-spec-slow-basis"></a>
<div id="spec:slow-basis" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22** (Slow bases and exclusion of fast modes). *For bounded $\lambda,\ell$ and small $a$, the exterior solution space of <a href="#spec:circular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:circular">[spec:circular]</a> has a two-dimensional outgoing subspace, locally holomorphic in $\lambda$. Its bases and parameter derivatives converge locally on $r\ge r_b$ to free outgoing bases as $a\to0$. Every homogeneous solution satisfying the top-derivative condition in Theorem <a href="#spectrum:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spectrum:classification">20</a> belongs to this subspace.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-slow-basis"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-slow-basis</summary>

*Proof.* Use $(v_h,v_h'/r)$ for each channel. The coefficient of $r^2$ in its $D$ equation is $$\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&-hi/2\end{pmatrix}.$$ Diagonalize by the eigenvectors with first component one, group the two zero eigenvalues, and apply Lemma <a href="#spec:block-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:block-reduction">21</a>. Writing $$\nu_h=-2(a+\lambda)+2hib,$$ the slow block is $\operatorname{diag}(\nu_h)+O(r^{-2})$, and the fast entries are $-hi r^2/2-12-\nu_h+O(r^{-2})$. For example, before diagonalization the bounded contribution to the second row is $-12(v_h'/r)-(b+hi(a+\lambda))v_h$; this verifies both constant exponents.

Factor out $r^{\nu_h}$ in a slow coordinate and $e^{-hi r^2/4}r^{-12-\nu_h}$ in a fast coordinate. The diagonal errors are integrable in $d r/r$. The off-block errors are also integrable if $m>|12+2\operatorname{Re}\nu_h|$ with a fixed positive margin on the parameter neighborhood. The factored equation is thus an integrable perturbation of a constant vector. On a sufficiently late half-line, its integral from infinity is a contraction on bounded vectors; prescribing its four limits gives four independent solutions. Continuation gives a basis on the entire exterior. This construction is holomorphic on a slightly smaller parameter neighborhood because the integrable majorants are uniform there.

<a id="spec:free-bases"></a>
Setting both fast limits to zero gives exactly the outgoing subspace. In fact the fast coordinates forced by slow coordinates are $O(r^{\operatorname{Re}\nu_h-m})$ before factoring, by the backward integral. Taking $m$ larger before each fixed number of differentiations gives <a href="#spec:slow-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:slow-condition">[spec:slow-condition]</a>. Increasing the reduction order does not change the limiting normalization: the additional transformations have arbitrarily high order relative to the prescribed normalization, and uniqueness of the backward integral applies on a common tail. Uniform exterior symbol bounds for the profiles and their convergence give convergence of these integral equations by dominated convergence; ordinary ODE continuation then proves the asserted local convergence. At $a=0$ the slow columns are, up to their nonzero normalizations, $$\label{spec:free-bases}
 r^\ell H(\lambda+\ell/2-hib,6+\ell,-hi r^2/4)$$ in their separate channels, by Lemma <a href="#free:slow" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:slow">4</a>.

<a id="spec:fast-derivative"></a>
If the fast limit $d_h$ in channel $h$ is nonzero, reconstruction and $k$ radial differentiations give the leading fast term $$\label{spec:fast-derivative}
 \partial_r^k v_h=
 (-hi/2)^k e^{-hi r^2/4}r^{k-12-\nu_h}(d_h+o(1))
 +O(r^{\operatorname{Re}\nu_h-k}).$$ The other channel’s fast contribution is smaller by $r^{-2}$; this follows from the off-block reconstruction. The same formula follows directly by successively applying $D$ to the reduced fast equation, whose leading multiplier is $-hi r^2/2$. For $\operatorname{Re}\lambda\ge-1/32$ and $k\ge7$, $2k>12+2\operatorname{Re}\nu_h$. Consequently the first term in <a href="#spec:fast-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:fast-derivative">[spec:fast-derivative]</a> dominates the slow term and its square is not integrable against $r^{11}\,dr$. Thus every fast limit vanishes. For any particular finite $\lambda,\ell$, the argument applies in a bounded neighborhood of those parameters; it does not require a uniform bound on the parameters of the mode being tested. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-spec-real-bound"></a>
<div id="spec:real-bound" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (Upper bound for the real part). *For all sufficiently small $a$, there is no nonzero regular outgoing mode with $\operatorname{Re}\lambda\ge4$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-real-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-real-bound</summary>

<a id="spec:upper-energy"></a>
*Proof.* Put $V_a=P/a$ and $\sigma=\operatorname{Re}\lambda$. Test <a href="#spec:real-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:real-system">[spec:real-system]</a> against $(\lambda+W)X$, and take real parts. The $J$ term has zero real part. Integration by parts, using <a href="#spec:divergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:divergence">[spec:divergence]</a>, gives $$\label{spec:upper-energy}
 0=\int\left\{(\sigma-c_*/2)(|\nabla X|^2+V_a|f|^2)
 +\nabla X^*(\nabla w)\nabla X
 -\tfrac12 W(V_a)|f|^2\right\}\mu\,dy.$$ Here the matrix $\nabla w$ acts on spatial indices and the expression is summed over the two components. Boundary terms vanish: after division by $A$, the slow bound is $X=O(r^{-2\sigma})$, with its symbol derivatives, and $\sigma\ge4$ gives all needed integrability.

<a id="spec:hardy"></a>
For completeness, the weighted Hardy inequality used here is uniform for small $a$: $$\label{spec:hardy}
 \int r^{-2}|f|^2\mu\,dy\le C\int|\nabla f|^2\mu\,dy.$$ Indeed, for $\rho=\langle r\rangle^{-4a}$, $\operatorname{div}(\rho y/r^2)\ge(10-4a)\rho/r^2$. Integration against $|f|^2$ and Cauchy–Schwarz prove the inequality with $\rho$; comparability of $\rho$ and $\mu$ proves <a href="#spec:hardy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:hardy">[spec:hardy]</a>. Cutoffs justify the calculation by density. Proposition <a href="#profile:existence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="profile:existence">13</a> gives $\nabla w\ge cI$ and $W(V_a)\le C a V_a$ on the inner ball, while its exterior positive part is $o(1)r^{-2}$. Since $\sigma-c_*/2\ge1$, these two negative contributions in <a href="#spec:upper-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:upper-energy">[spec:upper-energy]</a> are absorbed, respectively, by the pressure and gradient terms. A nonzero $X$ is impossible. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### An outgoing estimate for unbounded parameters

<a id="spec:cases"></a>
<a id="spec:schrodinger-radial"></a>
We next exclude loss of modes to large angular or imaginary spectral parameters. The main step is to express the outgoing condition as a relation between a mode and its radial derivative on one fixed exterior shell. Once this relation is available, interior energy and flux identities will exclude a regular mode. Consider a sequence with $a\to0$, $-1/32\le\sigma\le4$, and $\ell+|\operatorname{Im}\lambda|\to\infty$. Complex conjugation of <a href="#spec:real-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:real-system">[spec:real-system]</a> allows us to assume $\omega=\operatorname{Im}\lambda\ge0$. After extraction, one of the following holds: $$\label{spec:cases}
 \text{I: }\frac{\ell}{\sqrt{1+\omega}}\longrightarrow\infty;
 \qquad
 \text{II: }\omega\longrightarrow\infty,\quad
 \frac{\ell}{\sqrt\omega}\text{ is bounded}.$$ Set <a id="spec:high-definitions"></a>$$\label{spec:high-definitions}
 \begin{split}
 q_h&=r^{11/2}e^{hi r^2/8}v_h,\qquad
 L_\ell=(\ell+5)^2-\tfrac14,\qquad n_* =\max(1+\ell,\sqrt\omega),\\
 F_h&=r^2/16+b-h\omega-L_\ell/r^2,
 \qquad S=\max(1+\ell,\omega),\qquad E=16\sqrt S.
 \end{split}$$ The symbol $q_h$ here denotes an unknown function, not the first argument of $H$. Direct substitution gives $$\label{spec:schrodinger-radial}
 q_h''+(F_h+hi\gamma)q_h=\sum_j V_{hj}(r)q_j,
 \qquad \gamma=\sigma+a-3,\qquad |V_{hj}(r)|\le Cr^{-2}
 \quad(r\ge r_b).$$ The bound, uniform in the sequence, follows from the exterior pressure estimates; oscillations in $V_{hj}$ cause no difficulty below.

Choose a fixed $B_0>r_b$, and put $B_1=B_0+1$. In Case II take $B_0$ large enough that $L_\ell/B_0^2<\omega/2$ eventually. On this shell $F_h<0$ for both channels in Case I; in Case II $F_+<0<F_-$. In either case $|F_h|\asymp n_*^2$, uniformly on the shell.

<a id="stmt-spec-remote-data"></a>
<div id="spec:remote-data" class="lemma">

<a id="spec:remote-robin"></a>
**Lemma 24** (Outgoing data at the remote endpoint). *Every outgoing solution along this sequence satisfies $$\label{spec:remote-robin}
 q_h'(E)-hi\sqrt{F_h(E)}q_h(E)=O(E^{-1})|q(E)|.$$ The constant is uniform after discarding finitely many sequence terms.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-remote-data"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-remote-data</summary>

*Proof.* For $r\ge E$ use $(v_h,v_h'/r)$ again. Diagonalize the leading matrix $$\begin{pmatrix}
 0&1\\ h\omega/r^2+\ell(\ell+10)/r^4&-hi/2
 \end{pmatrix}.$$ Its eigenvalues are $$hi\left(-\tfrac14\pm
 \sqrt{\tfrac1{16}-h\omega/r^2-\ell(\ell+10)/r^4}\right).$$ The square root stays uniformly positive on $r\ge E$. Eigenvectors with first entry one give a uniformly invertible symbol change. Group the two plus signs as the outgoing block; take the two minus signs as separate blocks. These blocks have uniformly positive gaps. The bounded remainder has norm at most a fixed $C_0$, with uniform fixed symbol derivatives. The leading diagonal is purely imaginary.

<a id="spec:power-propagator"></a>
The order of choices is important. First choose $C>C_0+2$, enlarged if necessary to bound the Hermitian parts of the initial full and block systems. Next choose one fixed integer $m>2C+4$. Apply Lemma <a href="#spec:block-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:block-reduction">21</a> to this order. Finally pass far enough along the sequence that its finitely many $O_m(r^{-2})$ corrections have norm at most one on $r\ge E$. Both the full reduced evolution and each block evolution then have propagator bound $$\label{spec:power-propagator}
 \|\Phi(r,t)\|\le
 \left(\frac{\max(r,t)}{\min(r,t)}\right)^C.$$ This is Gronwall in $\log r$ after using skewness of the leading diagonal. No separation inside the outgoing block is required.

For each individual outgoing solution, all reduced coordinates have symbol bounds $O(r^\rho)$, where $\rho=-2(a+\sigma)<1$; their constants may depend on that solution and its parameters. In an incoming equation the $r^2$ leading coefficient is invertible. Solving for that coordinate and differentiating gives a gain of two powers of $r$; iteration gives the same gain for each fixed symbol derivative, up to the $O_m(r^{-m})$ forcing from the outgoing block. Thus the incoming coordinate is $o(r^{-C-1})$, with $m$ as chosen. This qualitative bound is used only to remove its terminal value in variation of constants at infinity.

After that terminal value has vanished, the quantitative estimate uses only <a href="#spec:power-propagator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:power-propagator">[spec:power-propagator]</a>. If $Y$ denotes all reduced coordinates, its incoming part at $E$ is at most $$C_m|Y(E)|\int_E^\infty(r/E)^{2C}r^{-m}\frac{dr}{r}
 =\frac{C_mE^{-m}}{m-2C}|Y(E)|.$$ Undoing the changes gives $$\frac{v_h'(E)}E=
 hi\left(-\tfrac14+
 \sqrt{\tfrac1{16}-h\omega/E^2-\ell(\ell+10)/E^4}\right)v_h(E)
 +O(E^{-2})|v(E)|.$$ The full coordinate size is bounded by $C|v(E)|$, since the incoming coordinates are small and the first entry of each outgoing eigenvector is one. Multiplication by the scalar defining $q_h$ proves <a href="#spec:remote-robin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:remote-robin">[spec:remote-robin]</a>; the $11/(2E)$ term and the difference between $L_\ell$ and $\ell(\ell+10)$ are within its error. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-spec-scalar-turn"></a>
<div id="spec:scalar-turn" class="lemma">

<a id="spec:scalar-value"></a>
<a id="spec:scalar-slope"></a>
**Lemma 25** (Scalar transfer and a turning point). *Fix one channel and write $F=F_h$. For $R\in[B_0,B_1]$, let $U$ solve $$U''+(F+hi\gamma)U=0,\qquad
 U(E)=F(E)^{-1/4},\quad U'(E)=hi\sqrt{F(E)}U(E).$$ If $F$ has a zero $r_0\in[R,E]$, define <a id="spec:turn-scales"></a><a id="spec:scalar-transfer"></a>$$\label{spec:turn-scales}
 \begin{split}
 A_0&=r_0^2/16,\qquad H_0=L_\ell/r_0^2,\qquad k_0^2=A_0+H_0,\\
 d_0&=(r_0/(2k_0^2))^{1/3},\qquad
 K(r)=(|F(r)|+d_0^{-2})^{1/2},\\
 H_f(r)&=\int_{\min(r,r_0)}^{r_0}\sqrt{-F(t)}\,dt.
 \end{split}$$ If there is no zero, put $K=\sqrt F$ and $H_f=0$. For $N_r(u)=K(r)^{1/2}|u(r)|+K(r)^{-1/2}|u'(r)|$, after taking a subsequence common to the two channels, there are $c,C>0$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 N_r(u)&\le C e^{|H_f(r)-H_f(t)|}N_t(u),\label{spec:scalar-transfer}\\
 |U(R)|&\ge cK(R)^{-1/2}e^{H_f(R)},\label{spec:scalar-value}\\
 U'(R)/U(R)&=\mathcal B_h(R)+o(n_*),\label{spec:scalar-slope}
 
\end{aligned}$$ where $$\mathcal B_h(R)=
 \begin{cases}-\sqrt{-F_h(R)},&F_h(R)<0,\\
 hi\sqrt{F_h(R)},&F_h(R)>0.
 \end{cases}$$ The first estimate holds for every scalar homogeneous solution and all $r,t\in[R,E]$. All left endpoint statements are uniform for $R\in[B_0,B_1]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-scalar-turn"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-scalar-turn</summary>

<a id="spec:turn-potential"></a>
*Proof.* The derivative $F'=r/8+2L_\ell/r^3$ is positive, so there is at most one zero. In the turning cases its defining equation gives $$\label{spec:turn-potential}
 F(r)=A_0((r/r_0)^2-1)+H_0(1-(r_0/r)^2),\qquad
 A_0-H_0=h\omega-b.$$ It follows that $k_0^2\asymp S$, $r_0\to\infty$, $r_0<E/2$, and $d_0\to0$. For the $h=-1$ turn in Case I, for example, $r_0^2\asymp \ell^2/(\omega+\ell)$, which tends to infinity precisely because $\ell/\sqrt{1+\omega}\to\infty$. The other turn has $r_0^2\asymp\omega+\ell$. In the no-turn case, necessarily the negative channel in Case II, $F\asymp S$ throughout $[R,E]$.

<a id="spec:phase-error"></a>
<a id="spec:wkb-error"></a>
Fix a large number $M$ and remove the interval $|r-r_0|<Md_0$. On either remaining interval choose $\operatorname{Re}p>0$ with $p^2=\operatorname{sign}(F)(F+hi\gamma)$. Use the two approximate solutions $$p^{-1/2}\exp(\pm\!\int p)\quad(F<0),\qquad
 p^{-1/2}\exp(\pm i\!\int p)\quad(F>0).$$ All integrals in this paragraph are over the outer intervals. Direct differentiation of <a href="#spec:turn-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:turn-potential">[spec:turn-potential]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \int |F|^{-1/2}\,dr&\le C,\label{spec:phase-error}\\
 \sup\frac{|F'|}{|F|^{3/2}}+
 \int\left(\frac{|F''|}{|F|^{3/2}}
       +\frac{|F'|^2}{|F|^{5/2}}\right)dr
 &\le CM^{-3/2}+o(1).\label{spec:wkb-error}
\end{aligned}$$ Here and below the constants multiplying $M^{-3/2}$ do not depend on $M$. To verify the estimates, on $[r_0/2,2r_0]$ use $|F|\asymp k_0^2|r-r_0|/r_0$, $|F'|\le Ck_0^2/r_0$, $|F''|\le Ck_0^2/r_0^2$ and $d_0^3=r_0/(2k_0^2)$. Integrating the powers $|r-r_0|^{-3/2}$ and $|r-r_0|^{-5/2}$ from $Md_0$ gives the stated error. Off this interval $|F|\gtrsim k_0^2$, with relative derivative bounds $C/r,C/r^2$, and $E\lesssim k_0$; splitting at $r_0/2$ and $2r_0$ gives a vanishing error and the bounded integral in <a href="#spec:phase-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:phase-error">[spec:phase-error]</a>. For no turn the same computations use $F\asymp S$ and give $o(1)$ in <a href="#spec:wkb-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:wkb-error">[spec:wkb-error]</a> on all of $[R,E]$.

<a id="spec:transfer-error"></a>
Here is a transfer justification retaining the error that will be needed below. Each approximate solution has residual potential $$\frac{(p^{-1/2})''}{p^{-1/2}}
 =\frac34(p'/p)^2-\frac12p''/p.$$ The approximate pair has constant nonzero Wronskian. Its transfer matrix in the $N$ norms is at most $C\exp(|H_f(r)-H_f(t)|)$: $K\asymp|p|$, and $\int|p-\sqrt{|F|}|\,dr\le C$ by <a href="#spec:phase-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:phase-error">[spec:phase-error]</a>. Variation of constants for the residual followed by Gronwall bounds the difference between exact and approximate transfer by $$\label{spec:transfer-error}
 (CM^{-3/2}+o(1))e^{|H_f(r)-H_f(t)|}.$$ Indeed the residual divided by $K$ has integral bounded by <a href="#spec:wkb-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:wkb-error">[spec:wkb-error]</a>; when $r,s,t$ occur in order, monotonicity of $H_f$ gives $|H_f(r)-H_f(s)|+|H_f(s)-H_f(t)|=|H_f(r)-H_f(t)|$. This removes the exponential factor from the integral inequality. With no turn the error coefficient is $o(1)$.

<a id="spec:positive-branch"></a>
At $E$, the prescribed data select the outgoing approximate branch up to an error tending to zero. On the positive outer interval, <a href="#spec:phase-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:phase-error">[spec:phase-error]</a> bounds its normalized amplitude above and below, because the absolute imaginary action is bounded. Consequently $$\label{spec:positive-branch}
 c\le F^{1/4}|U|\le C,\qquad
 \frac{U'}{\sqrt F\,U}=hi+O(M^{-3/2})+o(1).$$ This proves all claims in the no-turn case.

<a id="spec:airy-equation"></a>
In a turning case set $\xi=(r-r_0)/d_0$ and $Y(\xi)=d_0^{-1/2}U(r_0+d_0\xi)$. Since $F'(r_0)d_0^3=1$ and $\gamma d_0^2\to0$, its equation converges on every fixed $\xi$ interval to $$\label{spec:airy-equation}
 Y_\infty''+\xi Y_\infty=0.$$ The corresponding transfer on $[-M,M]$ is bounded in the scaled data norm, with a bound depending only on fixed $M$. Figure <a href="#fig:turning" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:turning">2</a> records these three regions.

<figure id="fig:turning">

<figcaption>Schematic of a channel with one turning point in the high-parameter estimate, with $R ∈ [B_{0},B_{1}]$ as in Lemma <a href="#spec:scalar-turn" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:scalar-turn">25</a>. The scalar solution is selected at $E$ by $U(E) = F(E)^{−1/4}$ and $U'(E)=h i\sqrt{F(E)}\,U(E)$. For a fixed large $M$, pass to a subsequence and take the high-parameter limit in the window using $Y = d_{0}^{−1/2}U$ and $ξ = (r−r_{0})/d_{0}$. The resulting Airy solution has constant nonzero flux, so it contains a branch growing towards $R$. Only after this fixed-window limit is $M$ allowed to tend to infinity to obtain the normalized logarithmic derivative $U'(R)/(\sqrt{-F(R)}\,U(R))\to-1$. Distances are not to scale, and the arrows indicate branch selection, not a graph of $U$ or a sign assertion for its complex values.</figcaption>
</figure>

Visual witness description of PDF page 29 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A horizontal axis labeled $r$ with marked positions $R$, $r_0 - M d_0$, $r_0$, $r_0 + M d_0$, and $E$, partitioned into three contiguous regions by vertical dashed lines. Left region ($r < r_0 - M d_0$) is a shaded box labeled "Forbidden region", "$F < 0$", with a leftward-pointing arrow labeled "growing branch to the left". Middle region ($|r - r_0| \le M d_0$) is a shaded box labeled "Airy window", "$|r - r_0| \le M d_0$", "$\xi = (r - r_0)/d_0$", and "$Y_\infty'' + \xi Y_\infty = 0$". Right region ($r > r_0 + M d_0$) is a shaded box labeled "Oscillatory region", "$F > 0$", with a rightward-pointing arrow labeled "outgoing branch". Below the axis, annotations read: "Normalized Airy limit: $\operatorname{Im}(\overline{Y_\infty} Y'_\infty) \neq 0$" and "$\text{nonzero flux} \implies \text{nonzero growing component on the forbidden side}$".
```


Choose one fixed $M_0$ so large that the error in <a href="#spec:positive-branch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:positive-branch">[spec:positive-branch]</a> is below its lower amplitude margin. At $\xi=M_0$, the scaled Cauchy data are bounded and their flux $$\operatorname{Im}(\overline Y Y')
 =\operatorname{Im}(\overline U U')$$ has sign $h$ and magnitude bounded below. A subsequence of these data converges, and continuous dependence of the scaled ODE gives one solution $Y_\infty$ on every fixed interval. Its flux is nonzero and constant, since <a href="#spec:airy-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:airy-equation">[spec:airy-equation]</a> has real coefficients.

We spell out why this gives the required *relative* margin on the forbidden side. With $t=-\xi$, the real equation is $y''-ty=0$. On a sufficiently late half-line it has a positive growing solution comparable to $t^{-1/4}\exp(2t^{3/2}/3)$. To construct one, prescribe the growing approximate data at a fixed large $T$ and use the residual transfer estimate with $p=\sqrt t$: the total error relative to the growing scale is at most $CT^{-3/2}$. The exact solution and its derivative are positive when $T$ is large. Its logarithmic slope $z=y'/(\sqrt t\,y)$ satisfies $$z'=\sqrt t(1-z^2)-\frac{z}{2t}.$$ This equation implies $z\to1$: for every fixed $\epsilon>0$, its derivative points into $[1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon]$ at both endpoints for sufficiently large $t$, and outside that interval it points inwards with magnitude bounded below by a positive multiple of $\sqrt t$. A decaying independent solution is obtained by multiplying the growing one by $\int_t^\infty y(s)^{-2}\,ds$. The growing comparison bound makes that integral finite and shows that this second solution and its derivative are negligible relative to the first. These real solutions have nonzero Wronskian. A complex multiple of the decaying solution alone has zero flux. Thus the coefficient of the growing solution in $Y_\infty$ is nonzero.

As $M\to\infty$ its growing contribution dominates its decaying one, and $$\frac{Y_\infty'(-M)}{\sqrt M\,Y_\infty(-M)}\longrightarrow-1.$$ In the approximate basis referenced at $r_-=r_0-Md_0$, the coefficient of growth to the left therefore has magnitude at least a fixed positive fraction of $N_{r_-}(U)$, for every sufficiently large fixed $M$ and subsequently late sequence indices. That fraction can be chosen independently of $M$: the normalized basis vectors have comparable size one at their reference point, and the displayed slope is separated from the decaying slope $+1$. For each such fixed $M$, $N_{r_-}(U)$ is also bounded below by a positive constant.

Transfer to $R$ now gives a growing term of size at least $cN_{r_-}(U)e^{H_f(R)-H_f(r_-)}$; the decaying term is negligible since the action difference tends to infinity. The transfer error <a href="#spec:transfer-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:transfer-error">[spec:transfer-error]</a>, relative to this growing term, is still $CM^{-3/2}+o(1)$ with $C$ independent of $M$, precisely because of the relative coefficient margin. For the value lower bound, choose one sufficiently large fixed $M$ and retain it; then $H_f(r_-)=O_M(1)$ and <a href="#spec:scalar-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:scalar-value">[spec:scalar-value]</a> follows with a positive constant. For the slope take an arbitrary sufficiently large fixed $M$, obtain error $CM^{-3/2}+o(1)$, and then let $M\to\infty$. This proves <a href="#spec:scalar-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:scalar-slope">[spec:scalar-slope]</a>. The transfer estimate follows from the two outer estimates and the scaled central transfer, using one fixed $M$. Both channels can use a common extracted subsequence. Finally, all estimates allow a moving $R$ in the fixed shell, where $|F_h(R)|\asymp n_*^2$. If uniformity there failed, the same argument at a sequence of offending endpoints would contradict the estimates. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-spec-boundary-estimate"></a>
<div id="spec:boundary-estimate" class="lemma">

<a id="spec:shell-robin"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Coupled outgoing data on a fixed shell). *After subselection, every outgoing solution along an escaping parameter sequence satisfies, uniformly on $B_0\le R\le B_1$, $$\label{spec:shell-robin}
 q_h'(R)=\mathcal B_h(R)q_h(R)+o(n_*)|q(R)|.$$ The error is an operator bound on the two-dimensional outgoing space.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-boundary-estimate"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-boundary-estimate</summary>

*Proof.* For each scalar channel let $D_h(R)=0$, $D_h'(R)=K_h(R)^{1/2}$. Lemma <a href="#spec:scalar-turn" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:scalar-turn">25</a> gives $$N_r(D_h)\le Ce^{H_h(R)-H_h(r)},\quad
 N_r(U_h)\le Ce^{H_h(r)},\quad
 |\mathcal W(D_h,U_h)|\ge ce^{H_h(R)},$$ where $H_h=H_f$ in that channel. The Green kernel with left Dirichlet data and the right Robin condition of $U_h$ is the product of $D_h$ on the left and $U_h$ on the right, divided by the Wronskian. It obeys the following two different action bounds: <a id="spec:green-actions"></a>$$\label{spec:green-actions}
 N_r(G_h(r,t))\le C K_h(t)^{-1/2}
 \begin{cases}
 e^{H_h(t)-H_h(r)},&r<t,\\
 e^{H_h(r)-H_h(t)},&r>t.
 \end{cases}$$ Each exponential is at most one, by monotonicity within its own channel. There is no comparison of $H_+$ and $H_-$. A right Robin derivative error $\beta_h$, with zero left data, similarly gives $N_r\le CK_h(E)^{-1/2}|\beta_h|$; this follows either from the same Wronskian or by using $D_h$ for the correction.

<a id="spec:coupling-norm"></a>
Let $\kappa=\min_{h,r\in[R,E]}K_h(r)$. Then $\kappa\to\infty$: at a turn it is $d_0^{-1}$, and in the no-turn case it is comparable to $\sqrt S$. Put $\mathcal N(q)=\max_h\sup_rN_r(q_h)$. The matrix forcing in <a href="#spec:schrodinger-radial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:schrodinger-radial">[spec:schrodinger-radial]</a> has Green norm at most $$\label{spec:coupling-norm}
 C\max_{h,j}\int_R^E
 \frac{t^{-2}\,dt}{\sqrt{K_h(t)K_j(t)}}
 \le\frac{C}{R\kappa}=o(1).$$ Also $K_h(E)\asymp E$ for both channels, so the right error <a href="#spec:remote-robin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:remote-robin">[spec:remote-robin]</a> has norm at most $CE^{-2}\mathcal N(q)$. The diagonal outgoing extension $q_h(R)U_h(r)/U_h(R)$ has norm at most $Cn_*^{1/2}|q(R)|$, since $K_h(R)\asymp n_*$ and $H_h(r)\le H_h(R)$. Variation of constants and absorption of <a href="#spec:coupling-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:coupling-norm">[spec:coupling-norm]</a> and the Robin error give $$\mathcal N(q)\le Cn_*^{1/2}|q(R)|,\qquad
 \mathcal N\left(q-\left(q_h(R)U_h/U_h(R)\right)_h\right)
 =o(1)n_*^{1/2}|q(R)|.$$ Taking the derivative at $R$ costs $K_h(R)^{1/2}\asymp n_*^{1/2}$. Together with <a href="#spec:scalar-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:scalar-slope">[spec:scalar-slope]</a>, this is <a href="#spec:shell-robin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:shell-robin">[spec:shell-robin]</a>. Every estimate used is homogeneous in the left data and uniform over the shell. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-spec-high-exclusion"></a>
<div id="spec:high-exclusion" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27** (Exclusion of escaping parameters). *There is no sequence of nonzero regular outgoing modes with $a\to0$, $-1/32\le\operatorname{Re}\lambda\le4$, and $\ell+|\operatorname{Im}\lambda|\to\infty$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-high-exclusion"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-high-exclusion</summary>

*Proof.* Use <a href="#spec:cases" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:cases">[spec:cases]</a> and Lemma <a href="#spec:boundary-estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:boundary-estimate">26</a>. All volume pairings in this proof have weight $\mu$, and $[\cdot]$ denotes its surface integral at the current sphere, including the factor $r^{11}$. On a fixed ball $\mu$ is uniformly bounded above and below, and the coefficients of $W$ and $L+\Delta$ are uniformly bounded.

In Case I pair <a href="#spec:real-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:real-system">[spec:real-system]</a> with $X$ on the ball $r<B_1$ and take real parts. The second-order term is $$\int(|\nabla X|^2+(P/a)|f|^2)\mu-\operatorname{Re}[X^*X'].$$ The boundary term has nonpositive real part, by <a href="#spec:shell-robin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:shell-robin">[spec:shell-robin]</a> and both negative signs of $F_h$. More precisely its real part is $\frac12\operatorname{Re}\sum_h\overline q_hq_h'+O(|q|^2)$, so its leading value is $-c n_*|q|^2$ and absorbs its errors. The absolute value of the remaining real terms is at most $C\|X\|_2\|\nabla X\|_2+C(1+\omega)\|X\|_2^2$. The angular barrier $$\|\nabla X\|_2^2\ge
 \ell(\ell+10)B_1^{-2}\|X\|_2^2$$ and $\omega=o(\ell^2)$ absorb these terms into an arbitrarily small fraction of the gradient term. Nonnegative pressure then gives a contradiction.

<a id="spec:case-II-normalization"></a>
<a id="spec:plus-vanishing"></a>
In Case II normalize $$\label{spec:case-II-normalization}
 \int_{r<B_1}(|X|^2+|\nabla X|^2)\mu=1.$$ Write the shell error in <a href="#spec:shell-robin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:shell-robin">[spec:shell-robin]</a> as $\varepsilon n_*|q|$, with $\varepsilon\to0$. Its leading diagonal has modulus at least $c n_*$, so $n_*|q|\le C|q'|$ on the shell. There is a point in its first half at which both $|q'|$ and $n_*|q|$ are bounded, by the shell integral bound furnished by <a href="#spec:case-II-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:case-II-normalization">[spec:case-II-normalization]</a>. For the channel $h=-1$, the energy $|q_-'|^2+F_-|q_-|^2$ propagates this bound to $B_1$. Indeed $|F_-'|/F_-\le C$, $F_-\asymp n_*^2$ on the shell, and the forcing in its equation has bounded integral, since it is at most $C|q|$ there. Differentiating the square root of this energy and applying Gronwall proves $$|q_-'|+n_*|q_-|\le C$$ from that point to $B_1$. The positive channel then obeys, in the upper-derivative sense, $$\frac{d}{dr}|q_+|
 \le-(\sqrt{-F_+}-\varepsilon n_*)|q_+|+C\varepsilon.$$ The distance from the chosen point to $B_1$ is at least $1/2$. Integrating this inequality with $\sqrt{-F_+}\ge c n_*$ gives $$\label{spec:plus-vanishing}
 n_*q_+(B_1)\longrightarrow0,\qquad q_+'(B_1)\longrightarrow0.$$

<a id="spec:flux"></a>
<a id="spec:cross-small"></a>
<a id="spec:all-boundary-small"></a>
We next remove the other boundary channel by a flux identity. The imaginary part of the pairing of <a href="#spec:real-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:real-system">[spec:real-system]</a> with $X$ is $$\label{spec:flux}
 \operatorname{Im}[X^*X']
 =\frac{w(B_1)}2\operatorname{Im}[X^*JX]
 +(\sigma-c_*/2)\int\operatorname{Im}(X^*JX)\mu.$$ To check the first-order term, integration by parts and <a href="#spec:divergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:divergence">[spec:divergence]</a> give $$2\operatorname{Im}\int X^*JW X\,\mu
=w(B_1)\operatorname{Im}[X^*JX]-c_*\int\operatorname{Im}(X^*JX)\mu.$$ Also the pressure-free equation $Lg=(\lambda+W)f$, tested against $g$, gives $$\label{spec:cross-small}
 \left|\int\overline g f\,\mu\right|\le C/\omega.$$ Its integrated gradient term, $W$ term, and boundary term are bounded by the normalization and the boundary bounds already obtained. The change to circular coordinates gives the exact cancellation $$\operatorname{Im}[X^*X']
 -\frac{w(B_1)}2\operatorname{Im}[X^*JX]
 =\frac12\sum_h\operatorname{Im}(\overline q_hq_h')(B_1).$$ Multiplying <a href="#spec:flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:flux">[spec:flux]</a> by $n_*\asymp\sqrt\omega$, and using <a href="#spec:cross-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:cross-small">[spec:cross-small]</a>, makes its right volume term tend to zero. The positive channel contributes $o(1)$ by <a href="#spec:plus-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:plus-vanishing">[spec:plus-vanishing]</a>. The outgoing relation for the other channel gives $\operatorname{Im}(\overline q_-q_-')=-\sqrt{F_-}|q_-|^2+
o(n_*)|q_-||q|$. Its sign and $F_-\asymp n_*^2$ imply $$\label{spec:all-boundary-small}
 n_*|q(B_1)|+|q'(B_1)|\longrightarrow0.$$

<a id="spec:f-small"></a>
Rellich compactness on the ball, including the full angular fields, gives a strongly $L^2$ convergent subsequence of $f$. The equation $Lg-Wf=\lambda f$ has uniformly bounded left side in $H^{-1}$ by the normalization. Division by $\lambda$, whose modulus tends to infinity, shows that the limit is zero. Thus $$\label{spec:f-small}
 f\longrightarrow0\quad\hbox{strongly in }L^2(r<B_1).$$

Finally put $S_d=W+c_*/2$ and test <a href="#spec:real-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:real-system">[spec:real-system]</a> against $S_dX$. The real part of the second-order and pressure terms is $$\begin{aligned}
 &\int\left(\nabla X^*(\nabla w)\nabla X
       -\tfrac12W(P/a)|f|^2\right)\mu\\
 &\hspace{8mm}
 +\frac{w(B_1)}2[|\nabla X|^2+(P/a)|f|^2]
 -\operatorname{Re}[(S_dX)^*X'].
\end{aligned}$$ All boundary terms vanish by <a href="#spec:all-boundary-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:all-boundary-small">[spec:all-boundary-small]</a>; the tangential derivatives cost $\ell\le n_*$, and $P/a$ is bounded on this exterior sphere. The $JS_dX$ pairing has zero real part. For $I=\int(S_dX)^*JX\,\mu$, <a href="#spec:f-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:f-small">[spec:f-small]</a> and one integration by parts in the term containing $Wf$ give $I\to0$. Further, $$2\operatorname{Im}I=w(B_1)\operatorname{Im}[X^*JX].$$ Hence $\omega\operatorname{Im}I\to0$, since $\omega=O(n_*^2)$ and the boundary values are $o(n_*^{-1})$. The remaining pairing $(\lambda-c_*/2)I$ therefore has real part tending to zero. We conclude that the displayed bulk integral tends to zero. The profile estimates $\nabla w\ge cI$ and $W(P/a)\le C$, together with <a href="#spec:f-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:f-small">[spec:f-small]</a>, force $\|\nabla X\|_2\to0$. The vanishing boundary trace and Poincaré’s inequality then force $\|X\|_2\to0$, contradicting <a href="#spec:case-II-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:case-II-normalization">[spec:case-II-normalization]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### The constrained compact limit

The preceding estimates keep every possible mode in a bounded set of angular and spectral parameters as $a\to0$. We now fix an angular degree and analyze that bounded region. The pressure coefficient $P_a/a$ becomes singular in the core; its limiting effect is the constraint $f=0$ there. We first prove convergence of the inverses with this constraint, then use an analytic compact pencil to transfer both the limiting roots and their algebraic multiplicities to positive $a$.

<a id="stmt-spec-inverse-limit"></a>
<div id="spec:inverse-limit" class="lemma">

<a id="spec:base-form"></a>
<a id="spec:inverse-norm"></a>
**Lemma 28** (Convergence of the constrained inverses). *Fix $\ell$ and a convergent profile subsequence. Let $R=2\sqrt Z$ be its limiting core radius, and fix a sufficiently large outer radius $B_2>r_b$. Let $E$ be the Hilbert space of $H^1$ pairs in this harmonic on the ball $r<B_2$, and let $$\mathscr Z=L^2(r<B_2;\mathbb C^2)\oplus\mathbb C^2,\qquad
 j_E X=(X,X(B_2)).$$ The map $j_E:E\to\mathscr Z$ is compact. Define $T_a:E'\to E$ by the coercive form $$\label{spec:base-form}
 \mathfrak b_a(X,\Theta)=
 \int\left(\nabla\Theta^*\nabla X+\Theta^*X
                         +(P/a)\overline{\Theta_f}f\right)\mu.$$ The dual $E'$ consists of conjugate-linear functionals on test vectors. Let $$E_0=\{(f,g)\in E:f=0\text{ a.e. on }r<R\},$$ and let $T_0:E'\to E_0$ be the inverse of the limiting form, with no pressure term and tests restricted to $E_0$. Then $$\label{spec:inverse-norm}
 \|j_ET_a-j_ET_0\|_{E'\to\mathscr Z}\longrightarrow0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-inverse-limit"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-inverse-limit</summary>

*Proof.* Rellich compactness gives the volume part of $j_E$; in a fixed harmonic the trace has only two scalar entries and is bounded, hence compact. Uniform upper and lower bounds on $\mu$ give uniform coercivity of <a href="#spec:base-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:base-form">[spec:base-form]</a>. Inverses exist by the Hilbert-space coercive form argument, also on the closed subspace $E_0$.

We prove a stronger sequential statement. Let $F_a\rightharpoonup F$ be any bounded weakly convergent sequence in $E'$, and put $X_a=T_aF_a$. Coercivity gives a bounded sequence in $E$ and $$\int P_a|f_a|^2\,dy\le Ca.$$ After extraction, $X_a\rightharpoonup X$ in $E$ and $j_EX_a\to j_EX$ strongly. Uniform boundedness of $P_a$ permits replacing $|f_a|^2$ by $|f|^2$ in the last integral, with error $o(1)$. Its weak-star limit $b\mathbf1_{r<R}$ therefore gives $b\int_{r<R}|f|^2=0$. This proves $X\in E_0$ without any pointwise lower bound on pressure near its moving transition.

Test first with $\Theta\in E_0$ whose first component is supported a fixed positive distance outside $r=R$. On that support the limiting amplitude is strictly below one, so $P_a/a\to0$ uniformly. The other coefficients converge uniformly on the ball. Weak convergence in $E$, and weak convergence of the inputs against this fixed test, therefore give the limiting inverse equation. Such tests are dense in $E_0$. Indeed the first component has zero outer trace at $R$; cutting it off across $[R+\epsilon,R+2\epsilon]$ costs, in addition to its shrinking collar norm, at most $$C\epsilon^{-2}\int_R^{R+2\epsilon}|\Theta_f|^2\,dr
 \le C\int_R^{R+2\epsilon}|\partial_r\Theta_f|^2\,dr
 \longrightarrow0.$$ The radial weight is comparable to a positive constant on this collar. The second component needs no cutoff. Density and uniqueness identify $X=T_0F$. Every subsequence has the same possible strong limit after $j_E$, proving $j_ET_aF_a\to j_ET_0F$ for weakly varying inputs.

If <a href="#spec:inverse-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:inverse-norm">[spec:inverse-norm]</a> failed, choose unit witnessing inputs and extract a weakly convergent subsequence in the Hilbert dual. The sequential assertion just proved and compactness of $j_ET_0$ would make both images converge strongly to $j_ET_0F$, a contradiction. This proves exactly the stated norm convergence; no operator-norm convergence into the full $H^1$ space is asserted. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-spec-compact-pencil"></a>
<div id="spec:compact-pencil" class="lemma">

<a id="spec:compact-equation"></a>
**Lemma 29** (The analytic compact pencil). *Near each bounded candidate $\lambda$, choose $B_2$ sufficiently large. The regular outgoing mode equation is equivalent to $$\label{spec:compact-equation}
 (I-\mathcal C_a(\lambda))z=0\quad\hbox{on }\mathscr Z,\qquad
 \mathcal C_a(\lambda)=j_ET_aK_a(\lambda).$$ These are compact holomorphic families, and $\mathcal C_a\to\mathcal C_0$ locally uniformly in operator norm.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-compact-pencil"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-compact-pencil</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#spec:slow-basis" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:slow-basis">22</a> and its large-radius normalization show that the values of a slow basis at $B_2$ form an invertible matrix, uniformly in a small neighborhood of the candidate parameter and for sufficiently small $a$. Thus the outgoing condition is $$X'(B_2)=\mathcal M_a(\lambda)X(B_2),$$ with holomorphic matrices converging locally uniformly to $\mathcal M_0$. Define $K_a$ on volume values and boundary entries by its pairing with $\Theta\in E$: <a id="spec:K-form"></a>$$\label{spec:K-form}
 \begin{split}
 \langle K_a(\lambda)j_EX,\Theta\rangle
 ={}&\int\left\{\Theta^*(I+(c_*-\lambda)J)X
                         +(W\Theta)^*JX\right\}\mu\\
 &+[\Theta^*(\mathcal M_a(\lambda)-w(B_2)J)X].
 \end{split}$$ The boundary bracket is at $B_2$. This is the weak equation obtained by integrating $-\Theta^*JW X$ by parts. It uses only the two types of data in $j_EX$. Its coefficients converge uniformly, and hence $K_a:\mathscr Z\to E'$ converges locally uniformly in operator norm. Lemma <a href="#spec:inverse-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:inverse-limit">28</a> proves the asserted convergence of $\mathcal C_a$, and compactness follows by factoring through $E$. For a kernel vector $z$ its lift is $X=T_aK_a z$, with $j_EX=z$. For positive $a$, smooth elliptic regularity gives a regular solution through the origin; its Robin condition extends it uniquely into the slow space. Conversely any regular outgoing mode satisfies the weak equation. These two maps are inverse on the kernels. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-spec-interface"></a>
<div id="spec:interface" class="lemma">

<a id="spec:matching"></a>
**Lemma 30** (Interface conditions and the limiting determinant). *The limiting kernel in <a href="#spec:compact-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:compact-equation">[spec:compact-equation]</a> consists of free slow exterior modes satisfying $$\label{spec:matching}
 f(R)=0,\qquad g(R+)=g(R-),\qquad g'(R+)=g'(R-),\qquad
 g(r)=c r^\ell\quad(r<R).$$ It is nonzero exactly when the two-column determinant of Proposition <a href="#free:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:main">3</a> vanishes.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-interface"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-interface</summary>

*Proof.* The form domain gives $f=0$ in the core and continuity of both traces at $R$. Testing the second equation without a constraint on its test component gives $Lg=(\lambda+W)f$ across the interface. Its right side is locally $L^2$ and has no surface delta, since $f$ is $H^1$. Radial integration therefore makes $\mu r^{11}g'$ continuous. Positivity and continuity of $\mu$ give continuity of $g'$. Inside the core $\mu=1$ and $f=0$, so regularity and harmonicity give $g=c r^\ell$. Piecewise ODE regularity supplies the one-sided derivatives involved. The first equation may contain a constraint reaction at $R$; the form domain does not impose any condition on $f'(R+)$. In particular we do not impose the generally false condition $f'(R+)=0$.

<a id="spec:free-determinant"></a>
Since $Q(R)=1$ and $Q'(R)=0$, the exterior circular conditions are $$v_++v_-=0,\qquad
 (\partial_r-\ell/R)(v_+-v_-)=0.$$ Using <a href="#spec:free-bases" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:free-bases">[spec:free-bases]</a>, the derivative of the argument $-hi r^2/4$ is $-hi r/2$. Consequently these two equations, after nonzero row and column scalings and a sign change in one unknown coefficient, have columns $$\label{spec:free-determinant}
 \binom{H(\lambda+\ell/2-hib,6+\ell,-hiZ)}
       {-H'(\lambda+\ell/2-hib,6+\ell,-hiZ)},\qquad h=\pm1.$$ This is exactly the determinant of Proposition <a href="#free:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:main">3</a>. The columns are nonzero by Lemma <a href="#free:columns" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:columns">7</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-spec-simplicity"></a>
<div id="spec:simplicity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 31** (Symmetry roots and first-order chains). *At the limiting disk root $j(b,Z)=0$, the only roots of <a href="#spec:free-determinant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:free-determinant">[spec:free-determinant]</a> in $\operatorname{Re}\lambda\ge-1/32$ are $0,1$ for $\ell=0$ and $1/2$ for $\ell=1$, each simple. The compact pencil $I-\mathcal C_0$ has a one-dimensional kernel at each such root and no first-order analytic chain.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-simplicity"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-simplicity</summary>

*Proof.* For every positive $a$ the first circular components $iQ$, $\partial_jQ$, and $(a-ib+D/2)Q$ solve the linearized equation at $0,1/2,1$, respectively, by differentiating phase, center, and blow-up time. These identities can also be checked without referring to a family of solutions. The first-component generator is $$\mathcal A v=i\Delta v-Dv/2-av+ibv-iD_Q(|Q|^{1/a}Q)v.$$ Phase covariance gives $\mathcal A(iQ)=0$; differentiating the stationary equation gives $\mathcal A(\partial_jQ)=\partial_jQ/2$. Commuting $D$ with $\Delta$ and using homogeneity gives $\mathcal A(DQ/2)=i\Delta Q$ and $\mathcal A(aQ)=-i|Q|^{1/a}Q$, whose sum equals $(a-ib+D/2)Q$ by the stationary equation. The term $-ibQ$ is a phase multiple and contributes zero.

At $a=0$ the same exterior identities hold with the power term deleted. The limiting phase, time, and translation fields satisfy <a href="#spec:matching" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:matching">[spec:matching]</a>. For the time mode its first circular component at $R$ is $-ib$, while its derivative is $-Rb/2$, which is real; for a translation it has value zero and derivative $-b$ times the appropriate first harmonic. These facts follow from the exterior profile equation $Q''(R)=-b$. The phase mode is immediate. They are slow by the free expansions; in the time mode the leading tail exponent cancels. They are nonzero: $b>0$, and the exterior profile is nonconstant. Proposition <a href="#free:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:main">3</a> gives exactly two, one, and zero roots with multiplicity in degrees $0,1,$ and $\ell\ge2$. Thus the displayed distinct roots exhaust the counts and are simple.

Suppose the compact pencil has a first-order chain, that is, $z_0\ne0$ and $$(I-\mathcal C_0(\lambda_0))z_0=0,\qquad
 (I-\mathcal C_0(\lambda_0))z_1
       -\mathcal C_0'(\lambda_0)z_0=0.$$ Lift it by $T_0K_0$, including the derivative term in the lift of $z_1$. Both first components vanish in the core. Differentiating the second equation shows that both $g$ components are harmonic there, since its new forcing is the leading $f$, which vanishes in the core. Both obey the interface trace and derivative requirements. At $B_2$ the correction satisfies the differentiated Robin condition $$X_1'=\mathcal M_0(\lambda_0)X_1
                 +\mathcal M_0'(\lambda_0)X_0.$$ The analytic slow basis extends these data to a first-order exterior solution. Therefore its two coefficients satisfy $D_0c_0=0$ and $D_0c_1+D_0'c_0=0$, where $D_0(\lambda)$ is the matrix in <a href="#spec:free-determinant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:free-determinant">[spec:free-determinant]</a>. A simple determinant zero has rank one and nonzero derivative pairing between a null vector and a conull vector. Applying that conull vector to the second equation is a contradiction. Kernel dimension one follows from the same matrix and the unique harmonic continuation into the core. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-simplicity-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-simplicity</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#spectrum:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spectrum:classification">20</a>.* First work in a fixed harmonic and a compact spectral neighborhood. The compact pencil is Fredholm of index zero. At a limiting simple root split its domain into the kernel and a closed complement, and its range into the image and a one-dimensional complement. The complement block is invertible, so elimination reduces the local kernel problem to a scalar holomorphic function. Its derivative is nonzero: vanishing would produce exactly the first-order chain excluded by Lemma <a href="#spec:simplicity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:simplicity">31</a>. Locally uniform norm convergence in Lemma <a href="#spec:compact-pencil" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:compact-pencil">29</a> gives locally uniform convergence of this scalar function and its derivative, by Cauchy’s formula. The argument principle on a small circle therefore gives one zero counted with multiplicity for small $a$. The exact symmetry mode places that zero at $0,1/2,$ or $1$, so it remains simple. Away from the limiting roots invertibility persists by a Neumann series.

If the classification failed for arbitrarily small $a$, select offending modes with $a\to0$. Lemma <a href="#spec:real-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:real-bound">23</a> bounds their real parts, and Lemma <a href="#spec:high-exclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:high-exclusion">27</a> bounds their angular and imaginary parameters. Extract a fixed harmonic, a convergent spectral parameter, and convergent profile parameters. The preceding compact-pencil argument gives a contradiction. This also gives the assertion uniformly over all the profiles in Proposition <a href="#profile:existence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="profile:existence">13</a>. Lemma <a href="#spec:slow-basis" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:slow-basis">22</a> then proves the homogeneous top-derivative assertion.

Finally suppose a regular vector $v_1$ with the top-derivative condition satisfies $(\mathcal A-\lambda_0)v_1=v_0$, where $v_0$ is one of the surviving modes. Choose an analytic exterior slow family $v(\lambda)$ with $v(\lambda_0)=v_0$. Differentiating its mode equation gives $(\mathcal A-\lambda_0)\partial_\lambda v=v_0$ on the exterior. Its differentiated slow tail has bounds $$|\partial_r^k\partial_\lambda v|
 \le C r^{-2(a+\operatorname{Re}\lambda_0)-k}(1+\log r).$$ For example, Cauchy’s formula on parameter circles of radius $(1+\log r)^{-1}$ gives these bounds from the locally uniform analytic slow construction. This tail is square integrable in order $k\ge7$ at the three nonnegative eigenvalues. Subtracting it from $v_1$ leaves a homogeneous exterior solution with the same top-derivative condition, so Lemma <a href="#spec:slow-basis" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:slow-basis">22</a> makes that difference outgoing. The original $v_1$ consequently satisfies the differentiated Robin condition and produces a forbidden first-order chain of the finite-$a$ compact pencil. This proves the claimed algebraic simplicity and completes the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:linear"></a>
### Linear decay and transfer to expanding tori

<a id="lin:potential"></a>
<a id="lin:symbol-hypotheses"></a>
Fix one sufficiently small admissible $a>0$ and a profile $Q=Ae^{i\phi}$ from Proposition <a href="#profile:existence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="profile:existence">13</a> for which Theorem <a href="#spectrum:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spectrum:classification">20</a> holds. All constants in this section may depend on this fixed profile and on $a$. No uniformity as $a\downarrow0$ is needed here. Put $P=|Q|^{1/a}$ and write the real-linear potential as $$\label{lin:potential}
 \mathcal Vv=P\left(1+\frac1{2a}\right)v
       +\frac{P}{2a}e^{2i\phi}\overline v,
 \qquad
 \mathcal A=i\Delta-\frac12y\cdot\nabla-a+ib-i\mathcal V.$$ The profile estimates give, for every nonnegative integer $j$, $$\label{lin:symbol-hypotheses}
 |\nabla^j Q(y)|\le C_j\langle y\rangle^{-2a-j},\qquad
 |\nabla^j\mathcal V(y)|\le C_j\langle y\rangle^{-2-j}.$$ The second inequality applies to each of the two coefficients in <a href="#lin:potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lin:potential">[lin:potential]</a>. We take $0<a<1$. The argument below uses the all-order estimates only up to a finite order, after that order has been chosen.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Spaces, products, and localization

<a id="lin:Y"></a>
Set $s_0=6-a$. For an integer $k>8$ to be fixed below, let $Y$ be the real Hilbert space obtained by completing complex-valued Schwartz functions in $$\label{lin:Y}
 \|v\|_Y^2=\|v\|_{\dot H^{s_0}(\mathbb R^{12})}^2
                      +\|v\|_{\dot H^k(\mathbb R^{12})}^2.$$ Homogeneous Fourier multipliers have their usual Plancherel normalization.
